## Physical unit reserves (2026-10-13)

`logistics.rs` ports all twelve exported reserve helpers from `shared/logistics.js`. `LogisticsState` stores only the optional reserve record in a `BTreeMap` keyed by unit ID. Owners, type names, positions, hit points and movement remain in their existing state; calls borrow the few unit facts they need. Definition metadata comes from the compiled unit catalog. Reserve constants retain the pinned source values and are checked directly against its `LOGISTICS` export.

Initialization preserves the source eligibility checks and never refills an existing reserve record. Ammo is spent in whole projectiles; supply stores hold ammo credits, including paid fractional credit until a complete projectile can be issued. Provisions, ordinary fuel, emergency fuel, warning time and shortage/recovery latches follow the original operation order. Non-finite or negative count/time/stock inputs receive the source's zero-clamping behavior where its helpers apply that rule. Metadata lookup errors occur before callers mutate reserve state. The numeric definition adapter and owner-controlled reserve access are not arbitrary JavaScript coercion or untrusted-save validation; a persistence loader must validate its own records.

The original JavaScript functions provide 60 enablement contexts, 118 weapon definitions, 708 initialization cases and 44 traces containing 618 exact reserve/stock/query steps. Tests include all 47 unit definitions, fractional volleys and credit, the credit epsilon, exhaustion and recovery boundaries, zero/invalid quantities, riding, actual powered travel time, repeat initialization and stable unit-ID ordering. Convoy routing, stores in the world, territory supply, retreat/path actions, persistence and private snapshot delivery are separate work. This module does not enable logistics in a match or change `Game`, the ABI or the rules hash.

## Weather planning and public helpers (2026-10-13)

`weather.rs` ports map mood/default selection, seeded weather plans, the single planned transition, warning rows, effect descriptions, AI caution and the presentation road mask from `shared/weather.js`. Weather multipliers, their ordering and the warning interval come from the compiled catalog. Existing movement speed and sight functions are unchanged. Plan state is separate from `Game` and can be held by a future match aggregate.

The planner takes an explicit `u32` seed and preserves the reference's private three-draw mulberry32 sequence. It does not consume the new global match streams. A future match initializer can draw and record one plan seed; that integration is not implemented here. `WeatherPlan.at` is a simulation tick, including for map defaults, whose reference helper expresses its transition in seconds. Typed weather names reject unknown values; metadata and lobby-choice adapters retain the reference fallback to the map's default. Road masks require a nonempty rectangular ASCII terrain view and describe the client's road appearance, not simulation road flags.

The fixture calls the original JavaScript helpers for 158 metadata cases, 5,682 seeded/explicit plans, 590 warning and transition rows, every weather description and AI caution value, and 231 synthetic road cases. It checks road-mask fingerprints for all 33 existing public authored-map projections, reusing those committed inputs rather than copying them. Local focused tests compare plans, rows, masks and floating-point AI caution bits exactly. Living rain, wet-ground changes, full match initialization, server snapshots and client integration remain outside this slice.

## Live weather phase

`weather_runtime.rs` supplies the late environment phase as borrowed views of the match's existing seed, wind, shower state and smoke fields. It owns no second copy of the match state. `environment_random` is the shared wrapping mulberry32 draw from the pinned `g.seed`: startup, wind, showers and later fire work must use that same seed, including when showers are disabled. The separate weather-plan seed remains independent.

The order follows `shared/sim.js`: apply the planned weather change near the start of the tick with the existing `WeatherPlan::step`; later, age smoke using the old rain intensity, consume two wind draws, drift every smoke cloud (including one whose lifetime just expired), then update showers, rain intensity and wetness. Rain holds the rainy flag and resets the shower timer; Snow clears the rainy flag without consuming a shower-timer draw; Mud pins wetness to one while showers can still change. Zero elapsed time still consumes the wind draws and can toggle an expired shower timer. One long step toggles at most one shower.

The return value only marks the every-ten-tick fire/flood phase and its `dt * 10` interval. The integrator must check for actual fire/flood work, perform it after weather, and remove expired smoke afterward, so smoke created by fire is not aged immediately. Fire spread, burning damage, flooding, terrain mutation and full Match wiring remain outside this helper. Nonfinite inputs, negative elapsed time and nonfinite results return an error before any borrowed state is changed.

The fixture adds exports to the original pinned module without rewriting its function bodies. It compares 59 sequences and 386 late-phase steps bit for bit, including all five fixed weather kinds, missing plans, disabled showers, 32 seeded random plans, Rain-to-Mud and Fog-to-Clear boundaries, rain/soak thresholds, wind clamps, expired smoke and long/zero time steps. A separate 400-draw check verifies the shared RNG states and results. These are deterministic correctness checks, not performance measurements.

## Deterministic match inputs (2026-10-13)

The core now has versioned random-stream state and a bounded order queue for the future match loop. The seed is recorded once. Terrain, weather, combat, off-map support and each AI seat derive a separate stream from that seed and a stable purpose key. Additional draws by one AI cannot consume another purpose's draws. Version 1 uses the wrapping integer operations in [Sebastiano Vigna's SplitMix64 reference](https://prng.di.unimi.it/splitmix64.c). It generates a 53-bit fraction for game probabilities and uses rejection sampling for bounded integers. This is simulation randomness, not a source for authentication tokens. Unknown snapshot versions and duplicate or unordered stream entries are rejected.

The queue consumes exactly one numbered tick at a time, including empty ticks. Callers assign an authoritative tick, issuer and sequence; accepted orders for that tick apply in issuer/sequence order, independent of their network arrival order. Admission policy is the caller's responsibility; a full queue rejects the next offered order. Capacity is caller-selected, and a rejected duplicate, past-tick or over-capacity order does not alter queued state. Snapshot data preserves the next tick and ordered pending commands. This replaces incidental arrival ordering inside the core; network admission and the complete command schema remain separate work. Neither component exposes a new C or WebSocket interface, and neither is a complete game loop.

Focused tests compare SplitMix64 with values from the compiled C reference, isolate purpose streams, resume streams and queues, permute arrival order, and reject invalid state. Integration with the full match simulation remains pending.

## Match deployment helpers (2026-10-13)

`deployment.rs` provides `spawns_for`, `spawn_distances`, `spawn_slots` and `win_vp` without constructing a match. Terrain is an explicit borrowed view of flags from the original authoring rows and optional levels, before building footprints or later match edits. Spawn filtering retains authoring order and includes Assault-only spawns only for Assault. Distance matrices and selected layouts use indices relative to that filtered list; the caller maps them back to the authoring spawn list. The VP goal reads `CFG.vpToWin` from the compiled catalog and scales by average team size.

Walking distances use east, west, south and north in source order. Entering a ford costs six cells, all other passable cells cost one; MOVE blocks entry, and adjacent levels may differ by at most one. Disconnected pairs use four times the straight-line distance through the core's deterministic `hypot`. Seven circular integer queues preserve the reference bucket insertion order while avoiding a separate queue for every possible accumulated distance. The input size bound keeps every reachable path below the reference's `1e9` infinity threshold.

Layout selection preserves the source's recursive order and pairwise score accumulation: enemy distances add to the score, allied distances subtract, and the nearest enemy pair breaks the tie after the 3% score filter. Both comparisons keep the source's `1e-9` tolerance. Four scans find the best score, best nearest-enemy distance, eligible count and chosen layout without retaining all candidate layouts. At most six players and six spawns are accepted, including shared starts when players outnumber spawns; the largest search is `5^6 = 15,625` layouts. A caller supplies either the first-choice selection or one finite random fraction in `[0, 1)`, drawn from its recorded match stream. There is no hidden draw or global random state.

Three focused tests compare all 33 authored maps plus eight constructed terrain cases, 957 layouts and 18 VP goals against the original JavaScript helpers at `e4e579a`. Cases include every six-player team partition, every supported player/spawn count, asymmetric ford distances, missing heights, cliffs, disconnected islands, shared starts and thresholds on either side of the tolerance. Invalid dimensions, buffer shapes, cells, indices, counts, non-finite/negative distances, score overflow and invalid random fractions return errors. Tiny maps are allowed for component fixtures; the authoring-map loader enforces the game's minimum dimensions.

This is component evidence, not a completed match-start pipeline. The caller still normalizes mode settings, applies the Assault defender-spawn override and Horde/Tutorial shared starts, handles World home assignments, and creates starting units, buildings and wallets. The helpers do not change the map loader, `Game`, the ABI or wire messages.

## Rust lobby and session server (2026-10-12)

`rust/crates/server` now provides `ww2-server`, a standalone HTTP/WebSocket process. Its first slice owns rooms, human and AI seats, observers, lobby settings, token reconnect/takeover, latency ping, a public room directory and cleanup. It does not start or simulate matches. Unsupported match commands return explicit denials, so clients cannot mistake a lobby for a running game.

Axum and Tokio handle transport; pure lobby transitions take an explicit monotonic time and return recipient-specific messages. Each connection has a generation identity. A replaced socket can neither mutate the room nor disconnect its replacement. Tokens stay private and retain their UTF-16 code units during truncation, so two distinct tokens cannot collide through lossy Unicode conversion. Name display at a split surrogate remains a documented difference in the server README.

Host authority follows the reference, including a spectator hosting an all-AI lobby. Directory entries require a listed room and a connected human seat. Disconnected seats remain for ten seconds; empty rooms expire after sixty. Each client has a 64-message output queue, with a slow client closed using 1013. Incoming messages are capped at 16 KiB; the current transport sends uncompressed JSON.

The server accepts missing or zero `hello.proto` and refuses unsupported versions. It sends `lobby.proto: 0`; no rules hash is advertised before a rules core is linked. The pending in-match privacy proposals are unchanged. Binding keeps the reference `HOST`/`PORT` default of `127.0.0.1:3000`, with `WW2_BIND` and then `--bind` as explicit overrides.

The live harness compares 38 lobby/directory checkpoints against the real Node server and checks all 33 map-catalog entries. It also checks version refusal, malformed input recovery, explicit start denial and oversized-message closure. Nine Rust tests cover settings, authority, identity, cleanup, limits and configuration. This is lobby compatibility for the tested cases, not full protocol or multiplayer-match completion.

The server also embeds the client-safe projection of all 33 authored maps. The generator calls the pinned reference's `mapForClient` and refuses a changed source tree. The binary serves those JSON bytes without Node or a reference checkout; it contains no raw authoring map route. Mines and their hidden material rows, triggers and scenario scripts are excluded. These projections cannot initialize an authoritative match. All editor-header map reads return 403, and map writes return 405 until the editor and full-world loader are built. A second live harness matches 96 HTTP responses against Node, including both URL forms, invalid paths, methods and credential refusal; two focused Rust tests check the embedded data and routing. Static browser hosting, map editing, authoritative world loading, compression, operatives, game simulation, snapshots and persistence remain missing.

## Combat damage and suppression component (2026-10-13)

`rust/crates/core/src/combat.rs` evaluates projectile arrival damage and suppression from explicit combat inputs. It also provides role, armor-facing, veterancy, retreat-protection, bunker-scaling and recent-threat helpers. Unit definitions and constants come from the compiled catalog; technology modifiers reuse `economy::tech_mul`, and bearings use the core's deterministic math. No fields are added to `Game`.

The component returns ordered `CombatEvent` values instead of mutating an incomplete match state. Damage events retain health changes, rider reactions, last hitter, death impulse, retarget reset, hit source and time, anti-tank hit time, and shooter XP. Suppression events retain the shared suppression-set insertion, the capped suppression value and hit memory. Building damage deliberately replaces the earlier AP, retreat and veterancy calculation before technology, matching the source. Zero damage still has the source's rider and hit-memory effects; already-dead targets emit none.

Rider events are requests for the later transport integrator. In original cargo order, an existing rider is removed from all copies in the carrier's cargo, has `riding` cleared, moves to the centre of its nearest free cell using its own movement block mask, updates the spatial index, and receives at least 60 suppression. This normal disembark does not clear its orders, path or targets, cause injury, drown it, or emit a shot. A missing rider ID remains in cargo in the source; the request contract preserves that detail. Terrain placement and grid mutation are not implemented by this component. Suppression takes the already evaluated `coverMul` and `coverBehind` values and combines them in source order; terrain cover sampling remains separate.

`harness/combat-fixture.mjs` exposes private functions through an export-only loader and executes their unchanged bodies at reference `e4e579a` under x64 Node 24.21.0. Property proxies record the actual mutation order. Six focused tests pass in debug and release on the Mac against 2,861 helper calls, 741 damage cases and 226 suppression cases. Coverage includes AP and naval restrictions, buildings and structures, technology, retreat, veterancy thresholds, zero damage, already-dead targets, overkill XP, rider requests, retaliation and duplicate suppression. The generator verifies the pin and clean tracked reference and reproduces its fixture with `--check`. Strict core Clippy and formatting pass. This component does not fire weapons, select targets, choose collisions, sample terrain cover, apply its events, remove dead units or implement blasts; the affected combat parity rows remain in progress.

## Capture points and point supply phases (2026-10-13)

`objectives.rs` owns the canonical objective record and two standalone phases: sequential capture-point updates and point-supply connectivity. Ordered unit facts carry the live unit's owner, kind, position, HP, riding, retreating and forced-reserve state; player facts carry team, eliminated state and spawn. The capture pass records qualifying unit IDs and contest status before handling the first qualifying player's team, prerequisite ownership, neutralization and capture progress. It uses the compiled catalog's infantry definitions, capture time and radius. A prerequisite captured earlier in the same point list is immediately usable; a later prerequisite waits until the next pass.

Capture completion returns an ordered event instead of updating the story or notifying clients. The match integrator must stamp capture time and tally the event only while undecided, as `story::captured` does. Capture state itself is not gated by a winner in the source. Ownership, progress and point order preserve the source calculations; no winner or income rule is implemented here.

Point supply recomputes enemy control and reached cells from an explicit terrain view, player spawns and the current unit facts. It uses `SpawnPlacer::nearest_free` with the source's MOVE-only seed mask, then floods left, right, up and down through cells free of MOVE and VBLOCK with height steps at most one. The seed itself remains reached even if closed or vehicle-blocked. Armed enemy ground units close an 8 m circle, except within 16 m of held points; retreating, riding, airborne and dead units do not close it, while forced-reserve or eliminated-player units still can. A point is supplied when any reached cell lies in its 8 m capture square. These circular-control and square-connectivity checks intentionally remain distinct. Neutral points have their cut flag cleared. Team processing retains first-held-point order. No hidden cache or terrain mutation is used.

The checked API validates the complete input set before changing objective records; supply flags are committed after all teams finish. `harness/objective-fixture.mjs` executes the unchanged capture statements extracted from `step` and the original `zocClosed`/`supplyLines` functions at reference `e4e579a`, with x64 Node 24.21.0. Four focused tests compare 214 capture traces (1,165 checkpoints) and 223 supply states, including exact progress bits, capture events, closed/reached masks and cut flags. Cases cover contesting, allies, ordered prerequisites, every unit type, forced reserves, bridges, elevation, free-zone boundaries, blocked seeds and naval blockers. Debug and release tests, strict core Clippy and formatting pass on the Mac. Phase scheduling, disabled-mode handling, income, territory refill networks, match integration and winner/loss rules remain separate work.

## Compiled catalog and economy helpers (2026-10-12)

The initial `catalog.rs` catalog embedded 42 post-initialization reference tables, including all 47 unit definitions, using `include_str!` and a single `OnceLock` parse. Access needs no files, network or engine. Explicit number tags preserve Infinity, NaN and negative zero. `harness/rules-fixture.mjs` verifies the reference commit and tracked source before generating the catalog, and a fingerprint checks every compiled field.

`economy.rs` provides 11 stateless helpers: recruitment prices, faction compatibility, per-unit population weight, support drop population, building and builder lists, support and ability prices, required tiers, research prices and technology damage multipliers. Typed inputs admit the game's four factions and valid technology levels; unknown units/supports or impossible research states return errors. Ability overrides retain the reference's numeric values, including non-finite ones. Technology multipliers use the core's deterministic `pow`, consistent with the existing math decision.

The recorded expectations call the JavaScript helpers themselves. Tests cover 10 mode inputs, every unit/faction, 80 support cases, 640 ability cases, 480 research cases and 56,400 damage multipliers. Grouped fingerprints plus 3,600 direct bit comparisons keep the fixture at 304 KB. The initial catalog was 26.6 KB; the following section records its expansion to 46 tables.

The existing movement/vision `Rules` loader still accepts harness data. This catalog does not change the C interface or its formation-only `RULES_HASH`. At this stage, purchase execution, income, full army population counting, availability and placement were separate work; these helpers alone do not validate an order. The wallet foundation below adds income and population operations without validating purchases.


## Compiled simulation rules (2026-10-12)

`Rules::compiled()` now constructs the existing movement, vision and fog rule structs from the embedded catalog, without Node, caller JSON or filesystem access. `Rules::from_catalog()` accepts an already decoded catalog. The catalog parser still runs once through `OnceLock`; the constructor returns owned typed rules that callers can hold independently.

This step expanded the catalog from 42 to 46 tables. Four additions provide `TRAFFIC`, `LANE`, `CORNER_BULGE` and resolved `MOVEMENT_PROFILES`. The recorder calls the original `movementProfile(type, UNITS[type])` for every unit, so profiles for 13 ground unit types include the source's private fallback without copied constants. Unit and weather indices follow the recorded `UNIT_TYPES` and `WEATHER_KINDS` arrays. Terrain hit-point entries keep the lexical key order used by the existing fixture loader.

One equivalence test reuses the existing vision recording and its independent `load_rules` parser. It compares every typed field, optional value, list order and floating-point bit for all 47 unit types, all five weather kinds and the movement/vision settings. The existing movement and vision replay tests remain unchanged. This does not connect the server to the core, implement a full simulation or change the ABI. `RULES_HASH` remains limited to formation constants; compiling these additional tables does not expand its agreed scope.

## Wallet, population and income state (2026-10-12)

`economy_state.rs` owns each player's wallet, income readouts and VP. It borrows an ordered view of units, building queues, pending drops, capture points, depots and World regions. The player vector index is the slot. This keeps the operations independent of the legacy `Game` and lets a future match runner supply authoritative state directly. World node-to-region resolution belongs to that runner; this module requires explicit regions and does not infer territory from hidden global state. The catalog now contains 47 tables, including the original exported `WORLD_TUNING`.

Population includes owned field units even when their hp is zero but they remain in the unit collection, queues only on structures, and incoming support units until their drop becomes live. Caps use the selected army size. World caps subtract all team seats from owned regions, split growth among seats not marked out, clamp before army scaling, then round. An away seat still participates in that split. A recipient-only World cap is returned unchanged.

The income phase preserves the reference's order: the leading VP total is captured before the player loop, each default-mode player's VP changes during the loop, and catch-up reads the updated team total. `Mode::Default` represents the absent mode object used by normal Conquest. Explicit Conquest, Tutorial and unknown mode objects retain the Assault fallback. Classic and World use living-unit upkeep and finished, living, owned depots; World additionally requires the depot's region to belong to its owner's team and splits regional income among teammates not marked out, including away teammates. Away or out players receive no income. Starting balances, wave and kill rewards, capture, elimination and production are outside this phase.

Three focused tests compare 37 original-JavaScript traces comprising 217 income steps and 865 player states, including all four army sizes, the seven game modes, absent and unknown modes, cutoff points, mixed teams, queues, drops, depot changes and World territory changes. Three additional World cap cases cover the clamp, eliminated teammates and fewer owned regions than seats; recipient previews and invalid-context errors are checked too. An independent review found that grouping a city's base and bonus changed the last bit: with two ordinary regions followed by a city, the source returns `5.550000000000001` MP/s. The regression now records that original-source result, and the implementation adds each base and bonus separately in source order.

Invalid slots or region indices, duplicate unit IDs, unknown catalog names, non-finite input and negative dt return errors before a wallet changes. This is a deliberate typed-input boundary in place of unchecked JavaScript objects. The fixture calls the unchanged source income block and `worldIncome` at `e4e579a`; it is component evidence, not a running match or end-to-end mode proof. Full economy and mode rows stay in progress. The server, wire snapshots, ABI and formation-only rules hash are unchanged.

## Production and research ownership (2026-10-13)

`production.rs` owns one paid-job vector and head progress value per building ID, plus each player's `TechState` and ordered research jobs. Queue types and active/waiting status are derived from that one vector. Unit ownership, HP, construction progress and ordering are borrowed for every operation; positions and authoritative unit state are never copied into the production state. Wallets stay in `EconomyState`. A new state takes the initial technology values and starts with no building entries; `initialize_building` creates an empty queue with zero progress explicitly, and first use can initialize an entry lazily. Reinitialization preserves existing paid work.

Construction-mode recruitment in Classic and World preserves faction, naval, tier, currency, population and unit-limit checks before selecting a producer. It uses the canonical queues when calculating population. An explicit producer is honored, otherwise the shortest eligible own queue wins with source-order ties; ordinary queues stop at five jobs. The reference's queue selector does not check producer HP, although cancellation and training require a living producer. The component preserves that edge behavior. Access and lab queries separately preserve Horde shared facilities, while ownership of queued units and refunds stays with the paying player.

Training emits at most one completed-unit request per building per advance, resets progress to zero and discards excess time. The match runner must create and place that unit, apply rally orders and initialize other unit systems. Research advances first, pauses while its HQ or Armories are missing, and resumes with progress retained. Armories run non-tier jobs in queue order up to available capacity. Away players continue research; out players stop it. Existing research continues during Classic Sudden Death even though new requests are refused. The match runner calls `clear_queues` once at the Sudden Death transition to clear production with no refund, matching the source.

Paid job IDs are monotonic and stay within JavaScript's safe-integer range. Cancelling a waiting job refunds its stored original charge, keeps the head's progress and returns the refund for the caller's statistics update. The low-level `record_paid` hook records an already-paid job, for example World recovery, without debiting a wallet or doing purchase admission. A queue-only import preserves matching positional identities and charges, assigning zero-charge IDs to changed entries as `productionJobs` does. Import/recovery queues have an explicit 4,096-job resource bound; normal purchase queues retain the source's five-job limit. Invalid inputs leave canonical queues and wallets unchanged.

Two focused tests compare 12 original-source traces with 114 actions and 922 advance calls, including repeated 0.05-second progress, stored-charge refunds, queue reconciliation, cap/limit denials, missing and parallel labs, Horde access, World currency and Sudden Death. The oracle executes the unchanged production/research functions and command branches, with unit creation replaced only by typed completion recording. Completion placement, instant recruitment, building creation/destruction, statistics, persistence and public snapshots/ABI remain outside this slice. The server continues to refuse match start until the complete runtime is ready.

## Conquest recruitment transactions (2026-10-13)

`ProductionState::prepare_recruit` admits instant recruitment for absent-mode Conquest (`Mode::Default`) and explicit `Mode::Conquest`. It borrows ordered authoritative unit facts, canonical queues and the existing `EconomyState`, preserving faction/classic/naval/tier checks, then MP, Fuel, population and unit-limit denial order before maker selection. Prices, caps, population weights and limits come from the compiled catalog and existing economy functions. The first eligible living, finished own maker wins in source order unless `from` names a specific eligible maker. Invalid `from` returns `needs`; teammates' buildings do not serve Conquest purchases. Instant recruitment does not create production jobs.

Preparation returns a non-cloneable `PreparedRecruit` with an immutable request containing building ID, owner, kind and spend. It holds the wallet exclusively and borrows production and context until commit or drop, preventing wallet or canonical queue changes through those references. The caller stages placement, ID allocation and routing before consuming `commit`, then publishes the staged unit without further fallible work. Commit debits MP exactly once and returns `CompletedRecruit`; dropping the token after failed placement leaves the wallet untouched. This moves source payment into the caller's successful placement transaction without adding another wallet or copying authoritative positions/health. The caller must keep the authoritative facts behind its view stable and process recruitment serially. Statistics use the returned spend.

One focused test compares 266 commands executed by the unchanged original buy branch at `e4e579a`, covering all 47 unit types across four factions, tier and denial precedence, exact MP balances, maker order and explicit selection, dead/unfinished and allied makers, queues, pending drops, fractional population and army sizes. The oracle replaces `produceUnit` only with a completion recorder; it proves admission/payment, not placement. A second test covers aborted placement and malformed input, checking unchanged wallets and single commit. Construction queue behavior remains unchanged. Other modes return an explicit input error. Command-level winner/away/out/rate checks, placement, routing, statistics, public wire/ABI and server match admission remain with their owners.

## Authoritative ordinary-map loading (2026-10-12)

`map::AuthoritativeMap::from_json` accepts original authoritative JSON bytes supplied by its caller. The core performs no file, network or engine I/O. It validates ordinary legacy maps and version-2 layers, retains the complete original JSON, and exposes typed spawns, capture points, buildings, legacy triggers and normalized layers. This is separate from the server's public map projections. Those projections have already lost hidden mines, materials and scripts; the format has no provenance marker, so the caller must guarantee that the input is the original authoritative document.

`initialize_terrain` uses `Rules::compiled` and the compiled catalog to build physical flags, optional height levels, house kinds, cell HP, stone-bridge toughness, deterministic starting wear, trench directions and naval shoals. Mines remain a separate layer, so their presence does not replace the underlying physical flags. Material maps use ordered keys. `TerrainState::query_game` supplies the existing movement, vision and navigation query state, with players, units and structural sections empty. It does not create a playable match or initialize structural graphs, scenario execution, mode state or match randomness.

The first ordinary-map stage returned `Unsupported` for every `world` or `scenario` property. Generated World support is now described below; authored scenarios still return `Unsupported`, including empty values. Authored structures are validated by the structural slice below. Legacy timed triggers are retained and checked against their migrated scenario bounds, but no trigger executes. Invalid supported fields return field-specific errors, without promising the JavaScript's exact error strings. JSON input is capped at 16 MiB before parsing; this is a core resource bound, separate from the reference editor's 8 MB HTTP limit. No server endpoint or C interface changes in this slice.

The recorder checks reference `e4e579a838aabae3b1d9dadbc17a0a87147bc52d` and a clean tracked source tree. It executes the original `createGame` initialization statements up to height initialization, then returns before structural setup, starting units and mode changes. All 33 authored maps and 16 valid boundary maps match the original source document, normalized layers and materials, flags, heights, house kinds, HP and wear float bits, trench-direction float bits and shoal masks. Another 55 cases record validation rejection, and two record the unsupported property boundary. The structural slice changed the former empty-structures rejection into an accepted boundary case. The fixture includes the 1024 by 1024 size limit and Unicode string-length cases.

Issue #35 tracks this slice. The local workspace tests, strict Clippy, formatting and fixture regeneration checks pass on macOS. This is component evidence, not full `validateMap` parity, spawn-to-point reachability, mode behavior, match startup, structural destruction or Unreal integration. The affected map rows remain in progress until their complete behavior and integration checks pass. No performance measurement is claimed.

## Layered generated World loading (2026-10-13)

`AuthoritativeMap` now accepts original generated World maps through the existing in-memory JSON validator. `world_map::WorldMapInput` provides the native generator a typed handoff for cell characters, optional heights, explicit normalized layers, spawns, landmark building tags and geography. Absent typed layers explicitly selects the source's rows-only migration. The handoff represents generated terrain, geography and landmark tags, not arbitrary authoring documents with authored structures or triggers; those remain supported through original JSON. No client projection is consulted or repaired into authoritative input.

Immutable `WorldDefinition` retains region vector order, cell-space sites and bounds, one home index per spawn, optional names/kinds/biome labels/team hints, the membership vector, biome bytes, seed/version and uninterpreted extra metadata. Membership indexes the source region vector; IDs are unique but are neither sorted nor reassigned. Rail and waterway metadata are retained for later systems without validating or executing their algorithms. `World::from_map` keeps this geography apart from canonical mutable `Game` terrain. Team hints remain authoring metadata, not live ownership. Regions, player homes, capture progress, defenders, rail state and other World mode startup are not initialized. Ordinary `StartPlan::prepare` explicitly refuses World maps rather than constructing Conquest from them.

World validation preserves the source's 1-6 spawn and zero-point rules, 64/128 region counts, 64-cell span, optional per-cell membership with indexes -1 through count-1, unique IDs/homes, open objective sites and one home per spawn. The connectivity walk starts at the first objective, visits east, west, south, north, refuses MOVE or VBLOCK cells and height steps greater than one, and must reach every objective. This is the map validator's connectivity rule. The generator's stronger corridor and bridge-destruction checks remain separate. Huge/Massive dimensions are not inferred or forced by the validator, matching the source's acceptance of authored dimensions within 20-1024 cells.

Malformed World inputs now return field-specific errors instead of the previous unsupported boundary. Defensive checks additionally constrain region IDs/team hints to i32, seed/version to u32, bounds to ordered in-map integers, biomes to one byte per cell, and optional kind/name/biome to text. The source validator does not fully check those fields. Any present `world` field, including null or false, selects native World validation and is rejected if malformed; the source can treat those falsy values as ordinary maps. Typed metadata cannot replace reserved geography fields, and supplied normalized material maps must match the composed layers. Construction returns a fresh value only after validation; no existing World is partially changed. JSON input retains its 16 MiB bound. File reads are bounded separately in `simtest::map::load`, exposed by `simtest --map`; the core does no file I/O.

The existing authoritative-map oracle now records 138 cases with 58 exact static terrain states: all 33 authored maps, the earlier boundaries, four bare/dressed Huge/Massive generated worlds, five synthetic World boundaries and 23 new source validation failures. The former empty-World unsupported case is now a validation failure. All other 105 earlier case records remain unchanged. Lossless run encoding reduces fixture size while retaining complete input membership/biomes and exact terrain arrays, materials and float bits. Typed tests cover canonical layer transfer, malformed inputs and the ordinary-startup refusal. This is component validation/loading evidence, not a generated Match, running server, wire change or Unreal integration. Eight focused map/world/structural tests, two ordinary startup tests, strict workspace Clippy and formatting pass. An independent source review and rerun of the map/typed-handoff test binaries found no actionable issue. The external loader read a dressed Massive input into 524,288 cells, 128 regions, one home and 1,736 structural graphs; no mode or players were initialized.

## Static structural graphs (2026-10-12)

`AuthoritativeMap` now validates optional authored structures through `structures.rs`. The original limits apply to the authoring format: at most 2,048 structures, each with 1-128 sections, unique structure and local section IDs, distinct physical cells, positive health up to 100,000, known materials and up to eight neighboring support references. Every authored section must have an undirected path to a live anchor, and bridge anchors must touch a bank. The loader uses physical layers for footprint checks, so a hidden mine over a house does not erase that house's structural footprint.

`initialize_structures` returns a separate `StructuredTerrain` containing the terrain and full structural graph. It installs authored sections first, preserving their order and exact double-precision health, then traverses remaining house and bridge spans in the reference's order and divides them into chunks of at most 128 sections. Automatic sections inherit the terrain's float32 health. Their IDs, support order, anchors and material defaults match the reference; support links stay within each generated chunk. The authoring limit of 2,048 structures is not imposed on automatically generated spans, because the reference does not impose it there.

Vectors preserve source insertion order and ordered maps provide lookup by structure ID and cell. Full sections retain identity, cell, health, maximum health, material, anchor, support IDs, state and rubble fields. The pure `supported_sections` query follows undirected links from live anchors and excludes failed or nonpositive-health sections. It does not apply damage or execute collapse. `material_name` resolves a live section material before layer overrides and defaults; ground-only queries bypass sections and object materials.

`StructuredTerrain::query_game` populates the existing reduced `Game.sections` projection without changing `Game::Section`. Players and units remain empty. The original `initialize_terrain` and `TerrainState::query_game` still represent the earlier static phase, with no structural graph; this keeps their fixture meaning stable. Dynamic damage, falling bodies, repairs, rubble placement, scenario execution and playable match startup remain separate work. Base-building section registration is provided by the mutable World layer below. The generated World loader above adds World input support; authored scenarios still return `Unsupported`.

The source recorder checks the exact pinned commit and clean tracked files, then returns immediately after the original `setupStructures` call. Ninety-five maps exercise 59 accepted initializations, including all 33 authored maps, and 36 validation failures. The authored maps contain 574 structures and 11,955 sections. Every graph field, insertion order, material result and float32 cell-health bit matches the reference. Boundaries include 2,048 authored structures, 128 authored sections, automatic spans of 127, 128, 129 and 257 cells, authored/automatic overlap, hidden mines, tiny positive health and material overrides. Eight additional support traversals cover one-way cycles, dead or failed anchors and live damaged/burning sections. These are component checks, with no performance or dynamic-destruction claim.

### Legacy timed map triggers

`legacy_scenario::LegacyScenario::from_map` takes a validated `AuthoritativeMap` and the actual match's retained point IDs and owners. It returns no state when the map has no legacy triggers. Otherwise it builds the original version-1 definition with `legacy:i` IDs, match scope, public recipients, time conditions and once-only repetition. Startup records owners (normally -1 before capture), leaves trigger state empty and starts `nextExecution` at one. It does not fire zero-time triggers during construction. General `scenario` documents remain unsupported by the map loader.

The module keeps typed definitions separate from typed mutable state. A step receives an exact integer tick, converts it to source time with `tick * 0.05`, records point-owner changes, then evaluates triggers in authored order. Actions retain their order, including Say before Destroy when a legacy entry has both. Each executed trigger gets `scenario:n`; a Destroy request includes its action suffix. Repeated calls, skipped ticks and time moving backwards follow the original once-only behavior. The caller stops stepping after a winner and applies the returned actions. This module does not mutate terrain, append shot rows to a live match, handle reinforcements or evaluate other scenario conditions.

Typed state can be inspected and restored with validation; output adapters expose the original definition and durable-state keys for fixture and persistence consumers. Invalid restore data and ticks outside JavaScript's exact integer range fail before changing state. Ordered definitions and action vectors preserve execution order; ordered maps make state output deterministic without making key order part of execution. Point IDs beginning with `region:` retain the source's reserved World lookup behavior, even on an ordinary map.

`harness/legacy-scenario-fixture.mjs` imports the pinned original migration, setup and step functions without source edits. Its 22 cases include all five authored trigger maps, 14 valid cases and eight validator rejections. One hundred steps cover initialization, simultaneous and fractional times, the last exact integer tick, time jumps, same-tick calls, owner changes, missing points, reserved target names and maximum permitted trigger work. Empty text is rejected by map validation, including when combined with Destroy. Two Rust tests compare definitions, full durable state and ordered action requests, then exercise restore/replay and atomic error handling. These component results do not establish integrated match or terrain-destruction parity.

## Mutable authoritative world (2026-10-13)

`World` owns the canonical `Game` and the authoritative data absent from that query state: mine/material maps, surface wear, scorch/scar/state arrays, fixed shoals, fire and aid records, terrain journals and structural metadata. `from_structured` consumes the prepared terrain vectors into `Game`, without cloning them for each tick. The original terrain snapshot is copied once and exposed read-only. Section health, maximum health and failure live in `Game.sections`; separate metadata retains identity, material, supports, anchors and nonfailed appearance. Appearance is not inferred from health.

`mutate_cell`, `set_cell` and `set_level` follow the pinned mutation path. They preserve the independent mine overlay, material routing, float32 health/wear, trench-facing resets, fire removal and mine/aid/wreck cleanup. `Wreck` now has an optional settled cell so replacing a `Q` removes the correct wreck. Surface and object edits update the same canonical arrays the existing core queries read. Region/obstruction counters and `Game.terrain_version` follow the source triggers, including explicit same-height edits; the initial road and shoal state is retained. Typed changes accept one terrain character, a boolean mine flag and an integer height from -2 to 4. Counter exhaustion is an explicit error before mutation.

Each logged cell gets a stable index on its first edit. Later edits replace that durable row while appending a new transient row. `touch` also journals changes that do not cross a packed-state threshold; look-only rows do not advance the geometry version. Internal recipient registration backfills all existing indices, and every later row marks each registered recipient pending. Draining `new_cells` does not drain recipient state. The consumer explicitly acknowledges only rows it consumed and remains responsible for visibility filtering. These authoritative rows are not wire messages. The world keeps a journal even for ordinary maps so future consumers can share this producer; callers own its drain/retention policy. Flood candidates preserve insertion order, including source-generated off-map neighbor indices.

`register_building_structure` installs each footprint section with the original `building:<id>:<cell>` identity, wood material, anchor flag, health and ordered neighbor supports, then logs it. When a building replaces a prior section's cell lookup, the older structure still retains its section identity and values: its canonical health moves from `Game.sections` to detached historical storage. A section never has two mutable health copies. Reusing a building ID, invalid cells, duplicate footprint cells or invalid health is rejected before insertion.

The recorder executes the original initialization and mutation functions from a clean reference `e4e579a838aabae3b1d9dadbc17a0a87147bc52d`. Six traces cover 78 actions over synthetic cases and No Man's Land, The Great Bridge, Three Islands and El Alamein. Every intermediate state compares terrain arrays, float bits, materials, mines, health, section identity/appearance, versions, flood candidates, logs and recipient pending sets. A separate test verifies vector ownership transfer, snapshot independence and invalid insertion atomicity. Issue #62 tracks this internal foundation. Generated World Conquest, authored scenarios, active fire/destruction/falling simulation, wire/ABI changes and complete match startup remain outside this slice.

## Explicit Assault startup (2026-10-13)

`Match::new_assault` constructs the source Assault deployment through an explicit defender-team argument. It stores a canonical `AssaultState` containing the defender, first attacking team (or -1 when absent), map/default clock and initial command-bunker count. All attacker teams normalize to the first non-defender team in the canonical options and player tables. The startup allocator still runs in source order before a map's `defend` list overrides spawns cyclically for defenders and attackers. VP-only points are removed, original scenario identities retained and prerequisites remapped through the retained list. A prerequisite removed by the mode imposes no lock.

Defenders receive the source fortifications before skirmish bases: the floating-step trench/sandbag arcs, a scattered command bunker repositioned toward map centre, then the ordered flak corner/ring/swing search with elevation, bunker-distance and previous-flak checks. Troops, fortification actors and bases retain source ID order. Terrain edits use canonical World mutation and buildings use the existing production/footprint/structure ownership. Ordinary unit factory and checked publication guard initialize controls and preserve the explicit World local-actor policy. Conquest and World share a private `StartupProfile`; their public entry points retain their existing mode refusal and supplied random history.

Assault MP comes from the defender/attacker rules and receives the selected army factor. Source Assault never enables logistics, including when its lobby option is true, so the constructor normalizes that effective flag to false. Weather and layout consume the existing independent native streams; the recorder supplies those exact draws to the unchanged JavaScript constructor. No player-facing rule or value is changed. Invalid original team IDs are rejected before normalization, and a failed constructor returns no partially initialized Match. The first native run caught an overbroad scenario guard: legacy timed triggers on five authored maps already have canonical shared-shell state, so they remain initialized and are compared by the recorder. New authored scenarios and World geography are explicitly refused by this ordinary Assault constructor.

The source recorder covers 43 starts and 702 units: all 33 authored maps, all army choices, weather/tech/shuffle configurations, multiple original attacker teams, no attackers or no defenders, cyclic reserved spawns, dropped/retained prerequisites and open, edge, river, steep and cramped forts. A lossless intern table compacts repeated fixture rows and reconstructs every original observation. Focused native tests compare unit values, canonical combat/firing defaults, controls, production, technology, initial legacy trigger state, all terrain arrays, ordered cell logs/journals, structures, private building footprints, recipient pending cells and mode/player/objective metadata. Assault and Conquest/World startup, publication, recruitment and story debug regressions pass. The final union passes all 162 locked release workspace tests, strict workspace Clippy, formatting and release FFI. Independent source review covers the constructor, shared startup profiles and established metadata publication callbacks. Three-platform CI remains with the shared integration owner.

This is startup only. Generic `Match::new`, Conquest accounting/finish and the Conquest tick continue to reject Assault. Its clock is stored but not advanced here, and no Assault victory is claimed. Recruitment, story events, timer/bunker finish, authored scenarios and server dispatch require explicit mode integration in later checkpoints. The wire and ABI remain unchanged.

## Canonical match story callbacks and internal result (2026-10-13)

`Match.story` now owns the source's seven player counters: kills, losses, built, captures, MP spent, support calls and planes downed. It replaces the earlier capture-only vector. Capture events from the actual Match point phase update that same story alongside capture timestamps. There is no damage-total counter in the source, so none is invented. Damage experience remains combat metadata rather than a match-report statistic.

Typed Match callbacks record accepted spending/refunds, construction completion, support calls, plane downing and deaths. MP refunds are signed reductions of the original charge; Fuel or Munitions payments do not become MP spent. A death callback consumes an explicit retired-unit snapshot after cleanup, resolves enemy attribution through canonical teams, and records the latest structure, bunker and production-building locations. It neither removes a unit nor awards its bounty. A read-only ordered-death preflight checks all retired snapshots and staged counter values before cleanup publishes, copying only the at-most-six story rows. With exclusive Match ownership and unchanged story/roster/finish state, the same post-removal callbacks cannot fail. As in the source, callbacks count nothing after canonical finish. Call sites own exactly-once emission; repeated calls are not deduplicated.

Prepared story updates borrow only the canonical story slice. They validate all affected values and stage changes without publishing them. Recruitment can hold its exclusive economy token at the same time, commit the charge, then consume the story guard through an infallible commit. Dropping the guard keeps all counters unchanged. This provides an atomic boundary for resource and unit admission without storing a second stats record.

`Match::conquest_result` produces an internal completed-match report with winning team, canonical teams/names, source reason/location/tick, rounded story counters, existing timeline, VP goal and Conquest mode. It returns no completed report before finishing, and rounding leaves raw counters unchanged. Server display-name overrides, result serialization, audience selection and the closing hold remain outside this API. No public wire or ABI fields are added.

The original-source recorder calls unchanged story functions and actual Conquest point/finish phases on assembled game state. Seven traces cover 42 actions, comparing all seven raw counters, signed refunds, ally/neutral death attribution, fallen locations, repeated calls, winner gating, capture timestamps and every internal result field. Four focused tests pass in debug and release, including dropping prepared updates, disjoint wallet publication, ordered death preflight, atomic invalid changes and negative-half rounding. Finish, point-income and startup tests, strict workspace Clippy and formatting also pass. This is a canonical callback and result foundation. Resource, combat, support and construction event owners still need to call it at every accepted effect before full-story parity can be claimed.

## Native Conquest victory and finish state (2026-10-13)

`Match::apply_conquest_victory` runs the source's final ordinary-Conquest victory check on the canonical match wallets and objective state. Each team's VP is summed in player order; the first player whose team reaches the goal determines the winner. There is no ordinary-Conquest HQ or unit elimination rule. Already decided matches retain the first winner, reason, end position and tick while later simulation phases can continue.

The end position is the most recently captured point still held by the winning team, with original point order resolving equal or absent capture timestamps. No held point falls back to the map centre. Coordinates use the core's JavaScript-compatible rounding to 0.1 m. Finishing appends the exact ordinary-Conquest story sample (`t` and floor VP indexed by team, including empty team-ID gaps), records a VP finish and sets the existing game reveal flag. No wire message, recipient filtering or public ABI is changed.

`Match::sample_conquest_tick` provides the source's tick-1/every-200-tick timeline phase. `Match::apply_conquest_point_economy` derives the decided flag from canonical finish state; the earlier point-phase signature remains available for its existing differential fixtures and also respects canonical finish. Completed captures update per-player capture counts and capture timestamps only while undecided. After finishing, VP, capture story counts and timeline sampling stop, while capture ownership and manpower income retain the source's ending-hold behavior. Command refusal, combat/spending story counters, result transport and the server's six-second hold are not integrated by this slice.

The checked phase validates team identities across canonical players/configuration, array lengths, score sums, point owners/coordinates and exactly representable simulation/capture ticks before committing a result. A malformed later player cannot leave a partial finish after an earlier player's apparent win. Other modes are rejected. Twelve traces execute original `createGame`, point/income statements, story sampling, Conquest victory and `finish` code at the clean reference pin under x64 Node 24.21.0; 24 assembled-match checkpoints compare winner, rounded end location, tick, reveal, complete Conquest timeline fields, capture counts/timestamps and wallets. Three focused tests also verify invalid-state atomicity and continued simulation with immutable winners. Focused debug/release tests, the existing Match startup and point-income tests, strict workspace Clippy and formatting pass through the shared Cargo cache on the Mac. The complete combat and server match loop remains separate work.


## Authoritative environment operations (2026-10-13)

`environment::EnvironmentRuntime` applies circular terrain damage, section damage, ignition, burning, flooding and section-debris steps to the canonical `World`. It borrows the match tick, shared environment seed and next-ID allocator, actual unit collection order, maintained spatial grid, wind, rain and wetness through `Context`. Health, suppression, garrison and fire-escape paths change in `World.game`; no mutable terrain or unit-health projection is created. Ordered typed effects carry collapse/hurt rows and building `LastHit` updates for the parent match's metadata. The caller applies those effects and schedules the operations; this component does not create a match loop or expose a wire/C API.

`World.fires` is now `OrderedFires`: a vector preserves first insertion order while an ordered map holds membership and timers. Updating a timer retains its position; removing and reinserting it appends it. Burn takes a snapshot of that order before spreading, matching the source even when a failure removes a later fire. Every eligible neighbour consumes its shared-seed draw, including at zero dt or full rain. `Game::Smoke` gains optional remaining lifetime; existing vision-only clouds keep it absent, while ignition assigns the original 12-second lifetime and borrows the common next-ID allocator. The late weather producer can borrow these canonical fields directly.

A section's health and failed flag remain in `Game.sections` at its current cell, or in detached historical storage when a newer section replaced that lookup. Additional failure metadata stores impulse, failure/settlement time and direction. Support is evaluated over the original structure's section IDs, including displaced historical sections. A failed section clears its object, emits a material/direction collapse, evicts garrisons and kills eligible bridge occupants. Building damage shares the existing entity HP pool: blast cells are grouped in first-hit order, sorted stably by strength, and excess damage carries into the next cell. Contact IDs retain the source's last-2,048 FIFO; beam memory is an explicit reusable typed input.

`environment_debris.rs` ports body initialization, contact reflection, bounded stepping and settlement through core math. The runtime retains section bodies and uses actual swept cell, vehicle and map-edge contacts. Vehicle contact ties use the supplied maintained spatial grid's bucket order. Settled house debris adds material-preserving rubble at its landing cell and along its direction, excluding occupied vehicles; bridge bodies add none. The combined section/moving-wreck count enforces the source's 96-body cap, solving and settling overflow immediately. Existing moving wrecks participate in that count, but their producer, motion ownership, lifetime removal and settled obstruction updates remain separate work.

Operations validate supported numeric inputs and reserve conservative version/ID headroom before mutation. Circular hits accept finite coordinates within 2^30 metres, radii up to 4,096 metres, damage and scar multipliers up to 100,000. Section destruction also accepts positive infinity, as the source's wreck path requires it. Burn accepts dt up to 7,200 seconds; section stepping accepts up to 2.5 seconds. Ticks keep exact JavaScript integer representation including the 50-tick debris lifetime. Invalid unit kinds, nonfinite fields, out-of-range garrisons, exhausted path identity and malformed fire timers fail before world/seed/ID changes. World arrays, structure metadata and the maintained grid remain canonical producer invariants. The isolated debris helper bounds malformed public body states to 4,096 substeps and returns atomic errors. These are explicit native resource bounds, not new gameplay rules.

The environment recorder imports original helpers from clean pinned source, adding exports and returning after static setup only in an in-memory module. Forty-four traces contain 253 actions over all 33 authored maps and crafted cases. Comparisons reconstruct every full expected state from compact path patches and check terrain float bits, materials, mines, section/entity health, metadata, ordered fires, smokes, contacts, falling bodies, seed, IDs, versions, journals and cell rows after every action. Boundaries cover float32 storage versus the source expression result, repeated contacts, FIFO eviction, detached sections, shared building damage, blocked escape paths, rain/wet cutoffs, one-wave flooding, progressive burning and overflow debris. Four runtime tests also check invalid-input atomicity, exhausted escape identity and fire registry order. The separate pure-debris oracle compares 54 mass cases, 21 constructors and 540 body/row states across 23 traces; its error test checks invalid environments and bounded work.

General World Conquest, repairs and bridge construction, hole-digging/filling, combat projectile scheduling, vehicle wreck creation/stepping, normal dead-unit removal and rider handling, public visibility/snapshots and full match outcomes remain unfinished. The engineering rows stay in progress until their integration and presentation checks exist.
## Deterministic Conquest tick assembly (2026-10-13)

`Match::advance_tick` applies recorded Move, attack-move, Stop, stance, ability and ordinary Dig commands at clock T-1 before advancing to T. Building sections synchronize, the maintained grid rebuilds and flight prior poses are captured before the increment. Section/wreck debris, story sampling, the weather plan and vision follow. The path scheduler starts with the source's 4,096-expansion budget and retry limit 20. The core consumes no wall time.

The runner visits the live unit map in ascending ID order. Increasing allocator IDs allow a unit born during a building turn to receive its own later turn, matching the source live Map iterator. Suppression speed is captured at the start of each turn. Waiting orders prepare before timer decay, attack-move follows it, and owner-observed route searches run at the actual movement request. Weapon and ability timers decay once. Autocast, real ordinary field work and aimed ability execution run in that order before attack-move and movement; targeting and launches run immediately after that unit's movement and grid update. Tick preflight checks canonical field activity against the descriptor. Dig admission, current-goal caching, budget requests, retry-limit cancellation and accepted replacement commands use the sole input/work owners. The assembly normally merges the checked engineering provider `00b6448`, preserving its actual actuator and the later canonical input variants. The moving fact is a remaining path or displacement over 0.001 metres. Traffic steering still requires its recipient-memory adapter, and automatic reactions remain unavailable.

The late actor list is captured before crowd separation and infantry progress. Flights advance next, followed by linked grenade-ticket pruning and physical salvo timers. Living weather borrows the canonical seed, wind, rain, wetness and smoke lifetimes. Fire/flood runs every tenth tick, before expired smoke is removed, so an expiring cloud still blocks same-tick ignition. Environment LastHit effects update canonical combat attribution; other effects retain their order in the report. The separate assembly calls `burn_captured` with that late list and a complete current-unit validation context. Direct fire damage and list-based rubble effects exclude later births, while garrison eviction and live occupancy still read current units. The current connected phases create no actors after the late list; support strike and scenario integration remain unavailable.

Recovery captures the late base/depot/aid membership after smoke expiry. Cadence-ten healing and mine sweeping precede point supply. Prepared reinforcement follows point supply and the reserved network-refresh site, before ordered retirement; capture/income follows them. The combined point API retains staged point/wallet commits and its existing differential checks. A separate source regression inserts wreck terrain between supply and capture/income, proving that income retains the earlier cut flags until the next supply beat. Retirement preflights every captured unit's potential story facts before removal, including later garrison casualties caused by building destruction. The reviewed combat adapter owns removal, wrecks and bounty. The assembly additionally stages ability/recovery row and paid field-work checks for every potential captured removal. At each actual removal boundary it cancels the sole field descriptor against the final Unit and removes ability/recovery metadata before dropping Game state. This cleanup changes no health, position, route or terrain facts; it retains Combat for active flights. The composed native replay covers this cleanup. Ordered snapshots then update story and remembered routing before another query.

The unchanged pinned `step` recorder now covers eleven traces, 2,185 ticks and 120 checkpoints across all army sizes, queued command timing, fire/expired-smoke ordering and opposing rifle squads. The ten prior traces and their ordering case remain identical. The added real paid Dig trace completes four trench cells at ticks 61, 122, 183 and 244, comparing descriptor progress, terrain/front angles and spending/built story through tick 245. New traces admit an aimed grenade at the recorded boundary and compare its launch, ticket expiry, ability/recovery rows and contact damage through tick 35, and buy missing infantry models at home on ticks 40 and 80 with exact wallet/story charges. Friendly units hold position to isolate missing reactions. The combat traces supply the native recorded Combat stream to prototype random draws; environment draws retain the source shared seed. One casualty occurs at tick 483, earns the exact bounty and kill/loss story, and simulation continues through tick 800. Comparisons include positions, headings, paths, movement/weapon timers, health/suppression, wallets/income, point state, raw story, vision cadence and random-stream bits.

The coherent operative assembly is compiled at `3a40f7b40270ab0bd0087e84b4cf52230628e90b`: all 225 locked workspace release tests, strict workspace/all-target Clippy, formatting and the release FFI build pass on the Mac (arm64). All 73 affected debug tests pass at runtime head `4f428b38`; the final head only restores a missing vision-only `Smoke.remaining: None` test initializer, with no runtime or recorder delta. This is local evidence; it does not establish Ubuntu, Windows, Unreal or transport behavior.

The separate assembly normally merges the operative provider and identity guards, preserving checked buff/AP firing and all four combat/firing/ability/recovery publication owners. Initial preflight validates the canonical companion registry before boundary mutation; admitted joins are checked again before prior poses and clock advancement. Respawn follows the increment before debris. Each living operative receives exactly one early provider turn and continues before ordinary unit orders, suppression and timers. Operative is reported as connected during dead waiting frames too. Flights, salvos, grenade tickets, prior poses, claims, the global recorded queue and allocator retain their owners. The regression checks death retention, collection on tick two, continued admission through tick four and a previously queued command on tick five. Strict orphan admission is unchanged.

Research/training, reserves/withdrawal, ordinary reactions, recipient traffic steering, air/rail, riders, mines/crushing/wear, construction, shared entrenchment, support strikes/scenario actions, supply networking and convoys remain unavailable in this runner. Ordinary represented field work runs at its actual per-unit site; excluded fort kinds retain explicit boundaries. Ordinary jump/crouch targeting interoperability and operative grenade settlement remain outside this bounded proof.

The same successor calls canonical support cover/cooldown decay after projectile phases and before living weather. It always decays each player's raw cooldown, including negative values, and reads radio ownership before this tick's point updates. SupportTimers is connected while strike actuation remains unavailable. Initial capability admission refuses existing strikes and aircraft. The sole queue exposes a borrowed ordered tick range, permitting the runner to refuse any current-boundary Support input before consuming even an earlier command or changing wallets, cooldowns or the clock. Future Support keys do not block earlier ticks. No alternate queue or timestamp policy is introduced. Server match start remains refused; complete-match outcomes and transport are not established.

First native execution exposed two test setup/projection defects and a source-recorder alias: the retirement guard passed an empty timer map, the tick consumer added control rows absent from the ability/Dig oracle, and saved Dig cells referenced the live deque later consumed by work. The consumer now uses actual canonical timer rows and only the original recorded optional fields; checkpoint queues copy each tuple. All other trace values and runtime bodies are unchanged. The corrected Dig checkpoints retain four, three, two and one cells before completion, preserving the real source phase instead of its later mutated array.

Initial input preflight rejects without changing the tick. A failure from any connected phase faults the runner, because earlier phases may already have committed; later calls refuse to continue that partial state. No recovery or persistence format is implied. Public ABI, wire messages and pending privacy decisions are unchanged.

## Ordinary reaction tick source composition (2026-10-13)

A separate successor consumes the canonical reaction owner at its actual ordinary unit sites: reaction timer decay beside weapon/ability timers, automatic retreat before autocast, then reaction after work/aimed/attack-move and before movement. The read-only automatic-retreat candidate shares the original predicate with execution and pre-clock unsupported-exit admission. Accepted retreat cancels the paid field descriptor after route/counter checks, without a refund. Operatives retain their early continue and unchanged reaction timer.

The expanded unchanged-source recorder reproduces 19 traces, 2,474 ticks and 354 checkpoints. Eight additions protect spacing, fresh-hit cover/rally behavior, timer bits, the strict health threshold, retreat-before-autocast and paid-work cancellation before any cell executes. The source-written full native consumer compares these cases through actual `advance_tick`; independent composition review is clear and Vostro execution at `e14cde9` passes 64 affected debug tests, all 227 workspace release tests, strict Clippy, formatting and release FFI. The first native run repaired only lazy Combat-state observation and NaN pace comparison; live RNG, runtime and fixtures remain unchanged by those consumer repairs. This does not enable supply, carriers, aircraft, construction, mass projects, active support or full room simulation. [Call sites and bounds](docs/runtime/reaction-tick.md).

## Authoritative Conquest startup (2026-10-12)

`match_runtime::Match::new` constructs an owned initial battlefield from an `AuthoritativeMap` and typed `MatchOptions`. `StartPlan` selects spawn slots and initializes players, wallets, objectives and weather. `World` owns the live terrain and units. Production owns each building's queue/progress and each player's research state; the original options retain configuration, not another wallet or research copy. The grid is rebuilt after all startup placement. Legacy scenarios initialize their neutral point-owner history and empty execution state without firing zero-time triggers.

One recorded match seed initializes purpose-specific native random streams. Shuffle draws from Terrain, while Weather supplies the initial environment seed and an optional random weather-plan seed. Startup wind and shower timing use the shared environment Mulberry32 implementation, which keeps its advanced seed even when showers are disabled. The fixture controls the original module's external draws from those recorded native choices. This verifies startup component arithmetic without claiming that a complete native match repeats the prototype's global random stream.

Starting rifle/rifle/MG units are placed before bases. Base site search preserves cardinal first-visit order, the 4,000-cell bound, avoidance radii and the initial occupied-footprint exception. A custom map with no legal site uses the source's cleared starting pad or skips an overlapping base. Footprints rise to their highest level, stamp through the canonical world mutator and register building sections. Troops trapped by those stamps relocate through the existing nearest-free placement. Private building-cell ownership remains after a future unit removal; recipient pending state and the terrain journal include all startup edits.

The original `createGame` recorder checks the reference pin and clean tracked files. Its two observational captures preserve placement and random draws: one captures troops before bases, and one starts journal/pending observation before base placement. Sixty-three cases cover all 33 authored maps, three synthetic maps, one/two/six-player teams, all four armies, all weather choices, explicit and derived weather seeds, disabled showers, tech on/off and shuffled/fixed layouts. They compare 663 initial troops, 1,101 final represented units, 438 bases and 3,942 stable logged cells. Final flags, heights, cell HP and wear bits, packed state, sections, geometry versions, transient logs, memory pending indices, resource/support values, production/research defaults and legacy scenario state match the source. Untouched wear cells retain the independently initialized terrain values.

This is Conquest startup, not complete simulation. Other modes return errors. Logistics-enabled startup uses the ordinary supply initializer described below. The unit table still carries only the fields already used by the ported movement and vision code. Combat orders, abilities, air state, full spawning, tick scheduling, action effects, persistence, snapshots and server match hosting remain integration work. Disabled showers require the Map weather choice, matching the source's single disabled-weather option. No C ABI, protocol, reference pin or player-visible balance rule changed. The related parity rows remain in progress.

### Conquest point phases

`Match::apply_point_economy` connects the objective and income components to the actual initialized battlefield. Objectives use the canonical type from `objectives.rs`; optional reserves remain keyed by unit ID. The caller supplies the tick's original ordered unit IDs, so a unit produced later in the tick cannot capture early. Missing IDs represent units removed since the list was formed. Player out/away state comes from the existing wallets. Supply connectivity runs on the source's configured beat before sequential capture, then income uses the resulting held/cut points and the no-mode-object Conquest context. This avoids the explicit-mode income fallback used by other reference configurations.

Capture timestamps update only while the match is undecided. The phase returns capture events for the caller's story/results integration; it does not advance the clock or decide the winner. Temporary objective and wallet copies are committed together after all validation succeeds, so an invalid wallet cannot leave capture progress advanced. Missing unit construction progress projects to zero only in the finite economy view, where it has the same finished-building comparison result as an absent source field. The canonical unit retains its absent field.

Eight recordings call the original late supply/capture/income source block on actual `createGame` instances. Seventy-two phases cover all four armies, allied/enemy teams, sequential capture prerequisites, contested points, out/away players, interrupted supply, units spawned after the tick list was formed and capture after a winner. Player MP/VP/income bits, objective control/progress/cut/contested/on-point state and capture ticks match. A focused error check verifies both duplicate-unit rejection and failure after provisional capture leave all objectives and wallets unchanged. Tick scheduling, real orders/movement, combat, story application, remaining modes and full server match hosting are still separate work; this is integrated phase evidence, not a full-match outcome run.

## Terrain navigation regions (2026-10-12)

`navigation.rs` ports `shared/navigation.js`: chunk-local connected regions, directed portals, region routes and limited next-leg selection. It is the coarse prerequisite for fine A*, not a complete unit pathfinder. A `TerrainView` supplies only borrowed flags, heights and version/change information; the planner cannot consult hidden terrain through another game object.

Region edges and incoming IDs keep the reference insertion order. Dirty chunks reuse freed region IDs in the same order, and route ties use region IDs. This matters because route choices can depend on update history. Direct changes or up to 32 retained changes update the affected chunks; missing continuity or a new terrain identity causes a rebuild. The route cache keeps the reference's 128-entry bound.

`NavigationLeg` borrows the cache's labels while owning its allowed-region list. Rust therefore prevents the caller from updating that cache while the fine search uses its filter. This replaces the reference's instruction to use a filter immediately with a lifetime rule.

All 4,888 recorded queries match the reference's target, connectivity, limit flag, ordered region IDs and per-cell filter. The 35 scenarios include 277 terrain updates, 1,372 limited legs and 1,268 disconnected outcomes. The recorder rejects a wrong pin or dirty tracked source. No performance claim is made. Integration into movement remains pending; the standalone request scheduler is recorded below.

## Infantry and naval pathfinding (2026-10-12)
