The initial `pathfinding.rs` slice added fine grid A* and path smoothing over the coarse navigation planner. `PathTerrain` supplies the terrain view plus wall proximity, known mines, fires and live bunker positions; `PathRules` supplies wall and bunker costs. The search reads no `Game` and cannot substitute hidden world state for the supplied view. That slice supported infantry and shallow/deep naval profiles; ground vehicles, aircraft, rail units and structures returned `UnsupportedProfile`.

The implementation preserves the reference's float32 path scores, score-only heap ties, neighbor order, diagonal corner checks, hazard costs, blocked-start escape and smoothing. An exhausted expansion budget has a distinct result from an unreachable route. Rust owns reusable search storage beside the navigation cache. The old undirected `regionsFor` rejection is unnecessary after the stronger directed coarse route has succeeded, so the fine search does not duplicate that cache.

The original `findPath` produces 1,175 oracle searches across all 33 authored maps and crafted terrain cases: 914 found routes, 163 unreachable results and 98 budget exhaustions. Every waypoint and resolved endpoint matches bit for bit, as do expansion counts and outcomes. Repeated searches after mine, fire and bunker changes are included. The recorder verifies the exact reference pin and rejects tracked changes. Vehicle clearance, automatic recipient-view selection and movement integration remain separate work. The standalone request scheduler and retry policy are recorded below. No performance claim is made.



## Path request scheduling (2026-10-12)

`path_requests.rs` adds a deterministic scheduler around caller-supplied goal eligibility, path searches and unit updates. It owns the FIFO, the latest pending token per unit and retry counts. `current_goal` must apply the live-unit and per-order conditions from the reference; this module does not implement those combat and order rules or select terrain.

Each tick supplies its budget, retry limit, tick number and tick duration. Defaults match the reference: 4,096 searches and 20 failures. Older queued work runs first. Dead, changed and superseded requests are removed before the budget check, and a replacement request takes its new FIFO position. Drained requests set the retry timer to one second plus the tick duration; immediate requests set it to one second.

Retry identity includes request kind, numeric goal, knowledge version and unit incarnation. Direct path commands clear prior failures. Empty recovery results preserve the existing route and do not count toward dropping an order. A search can advance the failure statistics and still return a nonempty route after a fallback succeeds; that route is retained while the failure is counted, until the retry limit cancels the order. Callback errors are returned rather than treated as successful paths.

The fixture executes the original private scheduling functions and uses the reference tick constant. Explicit adapters supply eligibility and bounded search outcomes. All 606 action checkpoints match: 207 searches and 22 order drops, with queue order, counters, routes, timers, cleared order values and movement outcomes checked after each action. The source recorder rejects a changed pin or tracked source edits. The fixture reaches every branch in the scheduling functions. Real movement integration, complete `currentPathGoal` eligibility, knowledge-view selection and full-match testing remain separate work; MOV-40 and MOV-41 stay in progress.

The original `findPath` produces 1,175 oracle searches across all 33 authored maps and crafted terrain cases: 914 found routes, 163 unreachable results and 98 budget exhaustions. Every waypoint and resolved endpoint matches bit for bit, as do expansion counts and outcomes. Repeated searches after mine, fire and bunker changes are included. The recorder verifies the exact reference pin and rejects tracked changes. At that stage, vehicle clearance, automatic recipient-view selection, retry policy, request scheduling and movement integration remained separate work. No performance claim was made.



## Internal Rust recipient view (2026-10-12)

`rust/crates/core/src/client_view.rs` is an internal Rust decoder of the pinned reference protocol. It holds one recipient's received knowledge, resets on each start, applies held-unit deltas and fog XOR updates, and retains raw terrain rows and HUD messages. Inputs are validated before mutation, with limits of 16 MiB per message, four million map cells and 100,000 held units. Pending terrain-cell changes accumulate until drained. The transport owns resync requests and their rate limit.

The view exposes typed unit rows and retains complete raw rows and extra fields. Its terrain cache is the latest received row per cell, not composed terrain or placement-query parity. Raw seen and explored masks exclude rendering effects such as fading, temporary unit illumination and lifting fog after a match. Unknown message types leave the view unchanged.

`harness/protocol-record.mjs` captures complete, sanitized reference messages. `harness/view-check.mjs` reads back the pinned browser's unit state and fog masks and exercises its cell-application functions with rendering dependencies stubbed. All 1,236 expected reports cover two recipients in Conquest and World Conquest. `simtest --view` compares these received-state reports, including raw terrain-row caches. Gzip belongs to simtest, not the core. Reconnects, operative sessions, save/load and transport behavior remain outside these recordings.

The internal decoder landed separately in PR #45. PR #16 now wraps that same decoder with six C recipient-view calls and raises the interface version to ABI 2; the formation signatures and rules hash remain unchanged. Axel and Julio have explicitly approved these calls and their staged Unreal consumer update, as recorded in `docs/contracts/rust-unreal-abi.md`. PR merge remains pending. Axel reported Linux UE 5.8.3 build, guard, replay and live shadow checks passing against exact head `03a49ac`, as recorded in the ABI contract. That consumer source is unpublished; packaged clients and authoritative decoder adoption remain pending. The broader contracts, additional C readers and terrain/availability queries remain separate work.

## Pure projectile flight and contact helpers (2026-10-12)

`rust/crates/core/src/projectiles.rs` provides typed, standalone versions of all pure helpers in the pinned `shared/projectiles.js`: profile selection, flight position and velocity, launch solutions, box/body/naval-hull sweeps, segment distance, crossed-cell traversal and material ricochet response. The module has no game-state, renderer or networking dependency. Projectile and material constants stay beside these helpers for now; they are not part of the compiled catalog or the public rules hash.

The port keeps source arithmetic, x/y/z slab order, corner-cell order, strict earliest-hit comparisons, box-before-cap ties and bounded rotational pieces. Trigonometry and two/three-argument hypot use the core's own math; square roots use the same hardware operation as the math module. The checked API rejects nonfinite inputs, reversed bounds, negative radii/lengths and nonpositive explicit flight durations. Grid dimensions are limited to 2^26 cells per side with an area fitting `usize`; hull angle wrapping allows at most 4,096 full-turn reductions. These limits prevent malformed input from overflowing indexing or keeping a tick in an unbounded loop.

`harness/projectile-fixture.mjs` calls the original helpers with x64 Node 24.21.0, refuses a changed reference pin or tracked source tree, and records exact floating-point bits. Six focused tests pass in debug and release on the Mac, covering 941 oracle cases: all profile/material constants, precedence, zero/negative elapsed time, launch arcs, tangent and corner contacts, translating bodies, rotating naval hulls, grid-cell order and ricochet thresholds. Strict core Clippy and formatting checks pass. This is pure-helper evidence only. Combat firing, flight expiry, game-world contact selection, damage, terrain changes and random scatter are not connected yet; CMB-48, CMB-49, CMB-50 and CMB-160 remain in progress.

## Ground-vehicle pathfinding (2026-10-12)

Issue #34 extends the initial infantry/naval search to all 12 non-rail ground types, including the lorry and Kaiju fallback profiles. A private vehicle navigation cache expands blocked cells by the hull's turning radius and rejects destinations without that clearance. `PathTerrain` adds explicit observed wear, public wetness and road presence; `PathRules` adds ground-speed constants. Road, mud, wood, ford and churn costs refresh for each search, as do mines, fires and bunkers. Road-aware estimates and smoothing preserve the chosen road route. The caller still supplies the entire terrain view; no `Game`, hidden world or automatic knowledge selection is consulted.

`PathRules::compiled` and `PathRules::from_catalog` construct all path costs from the same catalog as units and movement, with finite-value and range validation before match startup. The path oracle checks all 11 cost values bit for bit and runs all 1,529 searches using the compiled constructor. This adds no tables and does not change the formation-only rules hash.

The footprint cache uses the block mask and radius, source storage and recipient identity, and explicit terrain versions. Direct changes or a complete history expand only the affected cells. Missing history or changed source identity rebuilds the footprint. Expanded cells retain first-encounter order from the supplied changes and each row-major neighborhood, because the coarse planner's region reuse and tied routes can depend on that order. A private update counter also invalidates the coarse cache when a source array is replaced at unchanged versions, even if the footprint allocation is reused. Storage addresses serve only as cache identity, never as an ordering key.

The extended original-JavaScript fixture has 1,529 searches: 1,108 found routes, 299 unreachable results and 122 expansion limits. All 1,175 earlier infantry/naval query records are unchanged; 354 vehicle cases add road and terrain costs, bridge and narrow-gap clearance, elevation, blocked goals, dynamic terrain, source replacement and reverse-order distant edits. Waypoints and resolved destinations match bit for bit, with identical expansion counts and outcomes. Cache regression checks also cover unchanged reuse, direct and historical changes, a missing-history rebuild and recipient identity changes. The recorder checks the exact reference pin and rejects tracked source changes. Core/simtest tests, strict Clippy, formatting and fixture regeneration pass on macOS; cross-platform CI remains the delivery gate.

Aircraft, trains and structures still return `UnsupportedProfile`. Automatic recipient-view selection, retry policy, request scheduling and movement integration remain separate work. MOV-27 and MOV-28 are in progress, since search fixtures alone do not verify vehicle driving or an end-to-end match. No ABI or wire interface changed, and no performance claim is made.


## Spawn placement and current unit initialization (2026-10-12)

`unit_spawn.rs` separates deterministic placement from constructing the fields that `Game::Unit` currently carries. `SpawnPlacer` takes a supplied terrain view and a scatter index. It uses the core's sine and cosine at `index * 2.4`, four metres from the player's spawn. Ground units stay in the origin's movement component; naval units bypass that restriction. The nearest-free search keeps the reference's east, west, south, north tie order and returns the clamped starting cell if no cell qualifies, even if it is blocked.

Only component membership matters to origin-restricted placement. The implementation rebuilds that component from the supplied flags and heights, including symmetric diagonal corner connectivity, rather than copying the reference's global numeric region-label cache. Placement has no random draws and no hidden map reads. No performance claim is made for repeated component rebuilding.

`initial_unit` resolves the supplied kind index through `Rules` and obtains `models * hpPer` from the catalog. It initializes every currently represented field, preserving zero-valued optional velocity/direction fields, absent caches, and NaN pace for the reference's undefined value. A fresh empty route starts with native `path_gen = 0`. The caller still owns ID allocation, owner validation and insertion into the unit table and spatial index.

The original module's `internals.spawnUnit` and the original nearest-free/region function bodies provide the oracle: 769 spawn projections and 513 nearest-free queries, covering all 44 non-air definitions and all 33 maps. Every represented field is compared through the existing independent Unit loader. Air-base placement is explicitly unsupported. Logistics, unrepresented combat and ability fields, mode-specific starting forces and the complete spawn pipeline remain outside this slice; MOV-68 remains in progress.

## Recipient terrain for routing (2026-10-12)

`route_knowledge.rs` composes a routing and cover view from explicit public authored terrain or ordered remembered cell rows. Observed mode starts from the supplied authored terrain, removes initial hidden mine markers and retains supplied shoals. World mode starts with unknown cells: open flags, zero height and wear, and no remembered mine or cover metadata. Applying rows never reads a `Game`, collects visibility or substitutes authoritative terrain. Live fires, bunker positions, roads and wetness enter through `LiveRouting`; cover queries take already filtered wrecks. Their producers remain responsible for visibility and public-data policy.

Each row carries a caller-supplied revision scoped to its cell. The same revision skips the row before interpreting its contents, matching the reference's row-object identity check. A fresh equal-content row still advances the Observed version. World advances routing versions only for changed flags or height, or its initial team-view binding; a repeated World memory version skips the whole pass. Both modes preserve input change order and a 32-record navigation history. Metadata retains material overrides when omitted, clears omitted sections and trench fronts, and stores remembered HP, wear and fronts at the reference's float32 precision.

`CoverBasis` records public or remembered house kind and toughness when the character and section data do not determine full HP. An active section's supplied maximum needs no fallback. Unknown World cells return `UnknownCell` for cover queries; an incomplete known-cell basis returns `MissingCoverBasis`. Routes can still use that cell's known flags. This is an explicit integration boundary: the reference inherits `house` and `tough` from its full game object, but a native producer must establish which of those values are public or remembered before supplying them. The module does not infer hidden house extents or silently assume toughness one for World cover.

The recorder loads the pinned original helpers in memory with additional exports and supplies their memory rows directly. It changes no reference files and rejects a wrong pin or tracked source edits. The oracle checks 93 snapshots, 270 infantry/vehicle/naval routes and 48 cover query sets across crafted Observed/World sequences and three authored maps. It covers row identity, layer composition, mine ownership and remembered removal, float32 cover metadata, material precedence, settled and moving wrecks, fires, wall updates, trim fallback, history rollover and changes to unused authoritative arrays. Separate tests check missing cover inputs and atomic rejection of invalid row batches.

The observed producer below now forwards full remembered rows in first-insertion order and retains stable revisions. World memory still needs a producer that preserves the resolved journal order. The current `ClientView` cell map and sorted change list are insufficient to recover that order. Receive order alone is not a general replacement: the reference's snapshot outbox can reorder repeated edits while its remembered map retains first-insertion order. If a wire adapter cannot reconstruct that order, its producer must retain or supply it explicitly. This remains a producer-contract decision; the module does not invent ordering metadata or change the wire format. This slice does not change that decoder or the ABI, build a World memory collector, select a recipient automatically, perform the bank step-back fallback, schedule searches or integrate them with movement. MOV-29 remains in progress. No performance claim is made.


## Internal observed terrain memory (2026-10-12)

`observed_memory.rs` collects an ordinary map's per-player memory from `World` cell logs and caller-supplied visibility facts. It replays pending log indices in ascending order, reading current cell data and filtering hidden mine metadata before comparison or storage. Equal rows retain their revision and acknowledge the pending entry before visibility checks, as the source does. Hidden mines can reveal a changed physical surface while staying pending; an unchanged physical character, height and packed state suppresses a hidden mine's metadata-only update. A new terrain/vision key permits replay. Reveal and skip-fog bypass ordinary cell visibility, but do not bypass footprint visibility or hidden-mine suppression.

`ObservationFacts` supplies cell visibility, mine knowledge/ownership and building footprint facts. `FootprintFact.identity` identifies the same live or ruined footprint object across its cells; its visibility result is cached for that refresh. Allied footprints are visible. Live footprints use the player's visible-unit membership; ruins use the caller's team visibility at the footprint's position. The caller must supply the original player/team eligibility semantics and keep one producer with that player. This module does not calculate fog, select an owner automatically or read unavailable combat fields.

Full rows retain first-insertion order when replaced. A separate outbox removes and reinserts a changed cell so snapshots receive the latest-edit order; draining it never consumes full route or reconnect reads. Accepted replacements alone advance a checked revision counter. Updates and acknowledgments are staged before mutation, so invalid rows or revision exhaustion leave memory, outbox, replay key and World pending sets unchanged. The initial terrain allocation identifies the map binding, and a changed map requires new memory. Route forwarding rejects mismatched dimensions, recipient identity or World-mode views and passes only the full remembered order to `RouteKnowledge`. Callers retain the original route-view tick/terrain/vision cache separately from the collector's terrain/vision key.

The recorder runs the original `observedTerrainFor`, `seenWorldCell` and `observedPathView` bodies from the clean pinned reference. It stops match construction after actual map/structure setup and replaces only the visibility predicate with explicit team facts. Three traces compare 117 actions: exact remembered row contents and revisions, pending indices, full/drained snapshot order, route flags/heights/HP and navigation history. They cover hidden mine discovery and removal, changed physical terrain under a hidden mine, owned mines, repeated-key reads, route-before-outbox reads, allied/live/ruin footprints, building cancellation, reveal, skip-fog, team changes and reconnect. Guard tests cover atomic rejection. No performance claim is made.

Generated World Conquest memory, real fog wiring, client receive-order reconstruction, snapshots and full Match integration remain separate. The collector does not change the public wire format, C ABI or draft privacy contracts. MOV-29 and MAP-100 remain in progress.

## Routing inside movement (2026-10-13)

The movement block now has `move_unit_with_routes` and `move_units_with_routes` entry points. They invoke the caller's route handler at the reference's actual recovery point, after traffic has selected its steering goal. Infantry stalls request budgeted recovery; vehicle stalls and traffic replans request an immediate search. The handler owns terrain selection, scheduling, errors and path replacement. A replaced path advances `path_gen`, preserving the identity checks used by lane and clearance caches. Existing entry points retain the disabled-search behavior needed by the frozen benchmark.

Four original-JavaScript recordings run real fine searches inside movement for infantry and tanks on passable and disconnected maps. Across 48 ticks, all movement fields, separation and infantry progress match. The recorder restores the three disabled search hooks in the existing workload and supplies an explicit observed terrain view; other workload exclusions remain. This verifies the fine-search-to-movement connection, not a full match loop, recipient knowledge selection, logistics or combat. The standalone scheduler is a separate integration step.

## The core's math (2026-10-12)

The core carries its own math (`rust/crates/core/src/math.rs`) instead of Rust's `f64` methods, which call each platform's C library. It ports V8's fdlibm (`src/base/ieee754.cc` as Node 24.21.0 compiles it) and V8's `Math.hypot`, plus JavaScript's `round`, `max`, `min` and `sign`, so every platform computes the same bits and they equal the JavaScript game's.

- **The reference runtime is x64 Node.** Clang builds V8's fdlibm for arm64 with `a * b + c` fused into one rounding, so Node on Apple Silicon gives different last bits from x64 Node for every fdlibm function (Measurements). Baseline x86-64 has no fused instruction, so x64 Node computes the C source as written on every operating system, and the core follows the source. Parity runs and fixtures use x64 Node 24.21.0: the Vostro, or the darwin-x64 build under Rosetta on the Mac. `harness/math-fixture.mjs` refuses to run on arm64.
- **`pow` uses V8's own fdlibm `pow`,** after JavaScript's special cases (exponent 2 is `x * x`, exponent 0.5 is a square root). Node's default `pow` calls the operating system's, so the JavaScript game can differ between operating systems; the core gives one answer everywhere. It differs from default Node on about 6 to 10% of inputs, in the last bit. A deliberate difference (`docs/parity/interface.md`, IF-17).
- **The JavaScript game is not reproducible across CPU types either:** a seeded match on Apple Silicon and the same match on an x64 machine can diverge at the first last-bit difference. Reference runs that must agree are made on x64.

## Vision and fog in the core (2026-10-12)

`updateVision` and `teamFog` are ported to `rust/crates/core/src/vision.rs` and `fog.rs` and give the reference's answers exactly (Measurements). The data they read is in `game.rs` (the state) and `rules.rs` (unit kinds and the vision numbers, loaded from the reference's own tables until the core owns them, IF-01).

- **The fog caches sit beside the game, not in it.** The JavaScript keeps the fog terrain tables, the kept cells of still sources and each team's fog on the game object. Here they are one `Fog` value next to the `Game`, so the fog pass reads the game and writes only its own caches. That lets each team's fog run on its own thread (`Fog::teams_threaded`): a unit's kept cells belong to its own team's pass, so the teams write nothing in common, and the results merge back in team order. The answers equal one team after another.
- **Smoke keeps its identity.** A kept source remembers the smoke clouds near it by object identity in the JavaScript, so a cloud that drifts does not make it look again. Each `Smoke` carries an id that stands for that identity. The harness codec does not keep identity, so a source loaded from a state file remembers clouds that match none of the game's, as in the reference after decoding.
- **Units are in ascending id order.** The reference iterates its units Map in insertion order; ids only grow and no unit is put back, so `Game::units` is a `BTreeMap` by id. The loader refuses a state where the Map is in another order.
- **Ported as the reference has it, for now:** the fog's sight walk ignores the woods depth rule that `los` applies (`docs/parity/combat.md`, limitations), and the vision pass still runs one team after another.
- **Not ported yet** (Conquest does not use them): Horde stragglers and the Kaiju shown through the fog, the remembered enemy buildings of Classic and World Conquest, World Conquest's guards and its visible-cell list.

## Formation spots and the first C calls (2026-10-12)

`shared/formation.js` is ported to `rust/crates/core/src/formation.rs` and gives the reference's spots bit for bit; `rust/crates/ffi` (`ww2-ffi`) exposes it to Unreal (`docs/contracts/rust-unreal-abi.md`, First calls).

- **Sorts follow V8 exactly.** `sort.rs` ports the whole TimSort that Node 24.21.0 runs for `Array.prototype.sort` (`third_party/v8/builtins/array-sort.tq`): runs, binary insertion, the run stack, merges and galloping. Every stable sort agrees when the comparator is consistent, but `facingSpots` rounds how far forward a unit stands to the centimetre, and `Math.round(0.5)` is 1 while `Math.round(-0.5)` is -0. Units a few millimetres apart then compare as tied one way round and not the other, and the order depends on which pairs the sort compares. With a plain stable insertion sort in its place, 2,000 of the fixture's 10,300 seeded cases came out different. The fixture reaches every branch of the merge code except `MergeForceCollapse` picking the lower pair, which the run stack's invariants seem to rule out. Other sorts in the port (IF-25) can use the same function.
- **The C interface takes no shortcuts with C values.** Booleans cross as `uint8_t`, so no byte from C is an invalid Rust `bool`; shapes cross as `int32_t` constants, and an unknown one is an error rather than a Rust enum with an invalid value. A null pointer with a zero length is accepted, because an empty Unreal `TArray` hands over a null pointer.
- **`ww2_formation_spots` reports a count.** The draft had none, but the JavaScript returns no spots at all for a line or block whose average slot times spread is NaN, or 0 with no drag, so the call writes `*out_len`.
- **The last error stays until the next failure,** like `errno`: a successful call does not clear it, and reading it does not change it.
- **The header is checked by a test, not written by the build.** cbindgen is a dev-dependency, so building the static library needs only the core. `tests/header.rs` compares the committed header with cbindgen's output and fails when they differ.

## Movement and crowd separation in the core (2026-10-12)

The movement block of `step()`'s unit loop and `separateCrowds` are ported to `rust/crates/core/src/movement.rs`, `traffic.rs`, `vehicle.rs`, `terrain.rs` and `separation.rs`, and give the reference's answers call by call (Measurements).

- **What the block is.** From the traffic step ("Global waypoints, voluntary traffic steering and swept contact are separate steps") through `updateGrid` and what follows it in the frozen workload: the dust flag, `still`, the end of a finished retreat and the vehicles' stuck count. The infantry stuck count runs after separation (`infantry_progress`), as in the reference. `move_units` also has the parts of the loop around the block that movement reads: which units reach it, the suppression speed factor taken at the start of a unit's turn, and the suppression, route and sprint timers running down.
- **Left out for now,** so a game that uses them differs from the reference there: path searches (a unit that stalls, or that traffic tells to find a new route, keeps its route and asks again in a second, which is what the frozen workload's patch does); logistics (fuel limits and fuel use, convoys, units forced back by shortages; the loader refuses units with reserves, a convoy or an operative rather than move them wrongly); crushing, ground wear and mud it makes, and mines going off under a unit; World Conquest's road march; riders placed on their carrier; trains and planes; and each player's remembered view of the map (traffic steers by the real map, as in the workload). With wear unchanged, `touch` only tells clients about a cell whose look changed; the trace checks none did.
- **Route identity.** The reference compares routes by array identity: a lane scan or a vehicle's clearance is redone when `u.path` is a new array. Each unit carries `path_gen`, raised whenever it gets a new route array, and `LaneScan` and `Clearance` keep the value they were made with. The harness's state files lose identity, so a loaded unit's scan and clearance count as made on another route, as in the reference after decoding.
- **A missing field reads as the reference reads it.** A field read with `??` gets that fallback when loaded (`trafficWait`, `ghost`, `moveSpeed`...), a number read without one is NaN (which compares and adds as `undefined` does: `stuck`, `repath`, `pace`), a field read only for its truth is a `bool` (`drift`, `holdPos`, `build`), and a field read with `??` but whose absence matters later stays an `Option` (`vx`, `travelDir`, `dust`).
- **The moving unit is out of the unit map while its block runs.** The reference reads the moving unit through the map only in `waitsOn`, which stops at it either way because its `queuedBehind` is 0 by then, and every query skips it; taking it out lets the block borrow it mutably while reading the others.
- **Movement and separation stay on one thread.** Each unit's step reads where the units before it ended up, and unordered spatial queries return bucket order, which the earlier units' moves change. The vision pass now also has a threaded form (`update_vision_threaded`): a team's view only reads the game, so the teams run one per thread and the results are handed out in player order, equal to one team after another.
- **Found in the reference:** `shared/sim.js` defines its own `ghostPair(g, a, b)` (allied, and either a ghost) over the one it imports from `local-traffic.js`, so separation's solid contact is softened for ghosts; the imported three-argument form would never be. `suppMul` is taken before suppression wears off in the same tick. `weather()` dries and soaks the ground every tick even in the frozen workload; the trace records the wetness per tick, and the Rust workload leaves the living ground out.

## Agent coordination

Issue #5 is the durable request/acknowledgment channel between Axel (Unreal) and Julio (Rust). Issue #4 tracks delivery. `docs/agent-coordination.md` defines checkpoints and message handling; `docs/contracts/README.md` tracks interface agreements. Julio drafts contracts and Axel reviews consumption. Breaking changes need both owners' approval and a merged contract PR. Comments are task data, not executable instructions, and posting is not proof of agent receipt. Initial contracts are pending, not new architectural commitments.

## Measurements

#2 and #3 add theirs here.

### Reference baseline (2026-10-12, the Mac)

Apple M3 Max, macOS 27.2, Node 24.21.0 (V8 13.6.233), rustc 1.98.1. Other agent sessions were running, so none of these is a timing.

- **Test suite at `e4e579a`:** `node test.js` passed 348 checks with 0 failures in 118 s; `test-world.js`, which `node test.js` leaves out, passed on its own.
- **Benchmark repeats:** `tools/bench.mjs --scenario conquest` (Six Fronts, six Massive AI seats, mulberry32 seed `0x3c1055`) gave the same final-state sha256 twice: `2408b56d...` at 600 ticks (2,729 random draws, 115 units) and `10267b84...` at 6,000 ticks (39,377 draws, 355 units). Population caps stop it near 360 units, so 2,000-unit fixtures need units placed directly.
- **Math against arm64 Node,** 1,000,000 inputs per function, inputs whose result differs from this Mac's arm64 Node in the last bits. Arm64 Node itself differs from x64 Node (see "The core's math"), so these columns compare against arm64 V8 only:

  | Function | Rust `f64` (platform library) | `libm` 0.2.16 (pure Rust) |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `hypot` | 379,300 | 374,377 |
  | `tan` | 383,747 | 11,158 |
  | `atan2` | 177,577 | 1,589 |
  | `exp` | 97,471 | 457 |
  | `cos` | 46,964 | 13,579 |
  | `sin` | 43,564 | 14,017 |
  | `log` | 19,364 | 1,724 |
  | `pow` | 0 | 96,431 |
  | `sqrt` | 0 | not tested |

  A Rust copy of V8's `hypot` formula matched on all 1,000,000. V8 computes `**` with the operating system's `pow`, so the `pow` column holds for macOS only.
- **Arm64 Node against x64 Node** (both v24.21.0, the x64 build under Rosetta): `Math.sin` at 200,000 points from -10 to 10 gave different last bits for 1,100 of them; the first is x = -9.9994, 0.5435175700783331 on arm64 and 0.5435175700783332 on x64.
- **The core's math against x64 Node,** 1,000,000 inputs in each of 26 ranges (`harness/math-fixture.mjs`; game ranges and wide ones for every function): 0 differences for `sin`, `cos`, `tan`, `atan`, `atan2`, `exp`, `log`, `hypot`, `hypot3`, `round`, `max`, `min`, `sign`, and for `pow` against Node run with `--no-use-std-math-pow`. Against Node's default `pow` (the operating system's): 97,320, 59,512 and 90,612 differences in the game, wide and integer ranges on x64, and about as many on arm64. The Vostro wrote a byte-identical fixture. The committed test checks 200,000 inputs per range plus 1,384 edge cases.
- **Spatial grid against `shared/grid.js`:** a recorded log of 2,000 operations on 300 units (moves, moves the index is not told about, additions, deletions, rebuilds) and 744 queries of every kind gave the same ids in the same order (`harness/grid-fixture.mjs`, `tests/grid.rs`). The test fails if unordered queries are sorted or if a rebuild skips re-sorting its buckets.

### Vision and fog against the JavaScript (2026-10-12, the Mac)

Apple M3 Max, arm64 Node 24.21.0, rustc 1.99.0 release build. Other agent sessions were running (load average 10 to 14 on 14 cores), so the timings are rough; a second run at 2,000 units came within 5%.

- **Parity:** `harness/vision-check.mjs` (JavaScript) and `simtest` (Rust) print identical reports for `bench-6000` (355 units) and the three frozen states: four passes each (as saved, two passes standing still, then with the kept cells and fog terrain dropped), each player's visible ids and each team's fog and kept cells. The fog on one thread per team gives the same report. Arm64 and x64 Node agree on these states: the passes call `Math.cos` and `Math.sin` only for ships and live recon flights, which they lack. The committed fixture (`rust/crates/simtest/tests/fixtures/vision.json`) adds those and other cases; breaking any of 28 rules on purpose fails its test.
- **Time for one vision pass plus the fog for all six teams,** every source worked out again (as when every unit moves), mean and p95 over 50 passes, in ms:

  | Units | JavaScript vision | JavaScript fog | JavaScript both | Rust vision | Rust fog | Rust both | Rust fog, a thread per team | Rust both, threaded fog |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | 400 | 1.02 | 6.17 | 7.20 (7.70) | 0.38 | 2.18 | 2.56 (2.89) | 0.51 | 0.87 (1.11) |
  | 1,000 | 2.68 | 10.63 | 13.32 (14.55) | 1.05 | 3.57 | 4.62 (5.00) | 0.81 | 1.79 (2.03) |
  | 2,000 | 8.07 | 18.08 | 26.16 (27.66) | 2.60 | 5.83 | 8.43 (8.98) | 1.27 | 3.80 (4.22) |

  With every source standing still (kept cells reused), 2,000 units: JavaScript 13.93 ms (p95 17.05), Rust 3.47 (3.96), Rust with threaded fog 3.05 (3.36).

### Movement and separation against the JavaScript (2026-10-12, the Mac)

Apple M3 Max (14 cores), x64 Node 24.21.0 under Rosetta for recordings, arm64 Node 24.21.0 and rustc 1.99.0 release for timings. Other agent sessions were running (load average 8 to 14), so the timings are rough; a second run at 2,000 units came within 3%.

- **Parity, call by call** (`harness/movement-trace.mjs`, `simtest --replay`): no mismatch over 200 ticks of each frozen state, 68,800 movement calls at 400 units, 188,800 at 1,000 and 388,800 at 2,000, plus every unit after separation and after the infantry progress count each tick (1,360,000 unit states). Every field the Rust unit holds is compared, floats bit for bit. No field changed between a unit's block and separation by code the port leaves out. The constructed scenario of the committed fixture (`harness/movement-fixture.mjs`) also matches over 150 ticks (31,800 calls). V8 coverage of all these runs reaches every branch of the ported JavaScript except `??` and `||` fallbacks for values these states always have, maps without heights, boats, off-map edges, a house cell with no structure cell, the reversing and failing variants of the clearance search, the lane tie on equal distance, and two branches nothing can reach (a squad cannot reach a sidestep point in one tick, and a separation pair always has the lower id first).
- **The committed test** replays 227 units for 3 ticks (636 calls, 480 KB). Breaking any of 56 rules in the Rust code on purpose (ground speeds, wet ground, cliffs, cover ranks, the clearance and slide, the bend lead, sprint and pace, traffic ranks, queues, lanes, sidesteps, passing legs, ghosts, separation's rules) fails it.
- **The frozen workload, mean (p95) ms per tick over 200 ticks.** Node runs `harness/sim-workload.mjs`, Rust `simtest --workload`; "threads" works the vision pass and the fog one team per thread. Vision and fog are per vision tick (one tick in four). Node's step includes code the port leaves out (targeting, reserves, medics, the living ground) and not the fog; Rust's step is the index rebuild, the vision pass, movement, separation and the progress count.

  | Units | Run | Step | Movement | Separation | Vision | Fog |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | 400 | Node | 2.94 (4.83) | 1.21 (1.95) | 0.67 (0.91) | 1.21 (2.01) | 7.34 (18.69) |
  | 400 | Rust | 1.02 (1.45) | 0.42 (0.53) | 0.47 (0.56) | 0.43 (0.49) | 0.95 (1.42) |
  | 400 | Rust, threads | 1.00 (1.28) | 0.44 (0.60) | 0.47 (0.53) | 0.24 (0.27) | 0.38 (0.51) |
  | 1,000 | Node | 9.61 (13.68) | 3.85 (5.95) | 3.20 (3.90) | 3.19 (5.29) | 13.82 (27.40) |
  | 1,000 | Rust | 4.01 (5.08) | 1.56 (1.85) | 2.08 (2.34) | 1.11 (1.23) | 2.57 (3.26) |
  | 1,000 | Rust, threads | 3.97 (4.69) | 1.64 (2.03) | 2.10 (2.41) | 0.51 (0.58) | 0.85 (1.03) |
  | 2,000 | Node | 25.01 (36.45) | 10.00 (14.74) | 9.13 (10.43) | 9.72 (12.85) | 22.63 (37.79) |
  | 2,000 | Rust | 11.47 (14.18) | 4.50 (5.63) | 5.98 (6.57) | 3.09 (3.49) | 4.94 (5.65) |
  | 2,000 | Rust, threads | 11.22 (12.89) | 4.70 (5.84) | 6.06 (6.74) | 0.98 (1.07) | 1.50 (1.70) |

  The two games drift apart over the 200 ticks, since each leaves out different code, so these compare like work rather than identical positions; the threaded Rust run ends in the same state as the single-threaded one (equal unit and fog hashes).
- **Where the Rust time goes:** a 4 s `sample` profile of the workload at 2,000 units puts 36% of the samples in spatial index queries, 7% in index updates and 7% in unit map lookups. The index keeps unit ids, so every candidate costs a `BTreeMap` lookup, and its buckets live in a `HashMap` with the default (SipHash) hasher. Movement is 2.2 times as fast as Node at 2,000 units and separation 1.5 times; storing units by slot is the next step for both.

### Sim test (#2): Node against Rust (2026-10-12, the Vostro)

The frozen workload of `harness/sim-workload.mjs` (Node) and `simtest --workload` (Rust) on `frozen-400`, `frozen-1000` and `frozen-2000`, 200 ticks each. The Vostro: Intel i5-12400 (6 cores, 12 threads), Ubuntu 24.04.5, kernel 6.8, `powersave` governor; x64 Node 24.21.0; rustc 1.99.0, release build; PR #14 (`5b01e00`). Three rounds alternating Node, Rust on one thread and Rust with threads; the table gives the median of the three. The machine was not idle: its load average was 0.76 just before and 3.2 to 4.0 during and after the runs, part of it other work, so a quiet-window rerun may come out lower on both sides.

Milliseconds, mean (p95). Movement and separation run every tick; vision and fog every fourth tick, and their columns are per vision tick. "Ported work per tick" adds movement, separation and a quarter of vision and fog: the average cost of what Rust has ported. Node's whole `step()` is in the last column for context; it also runs code the port leaves out.

| Units | Side | Movement | Separation | Vision | Fog (6 teams) | Ported work per tick | Node `step()` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 400 | Node | 1.88 (3.00) | 0.95 (1.44) | 1.58 (3.11) | 8.62 (22.47) | 5.38 | 4.32 (6.93) |
| 400 | Rust, 1 thread | 0.61 (0.74) | 0.62 (0.66) | 0.55 (0.59) | 1.60 (2.25) | 1.77 | |
| 400 | Rust, threads | 0.68 (0.94) | 0.68 (0.90) | 0.30 (0.38) | 0.73 (1.18) | 1.62 | |
| 1,000 | Node | 5.62 (8.89) | 4.23 (6.34) | 4.04 (7.47) | 14.89 (32.40) | 14.59 | 13.12 (21.14) |
| 1,000 | Rust, 1 thread | 2.39 (2.99) | 2.89 (3.12) | 1.54 (1.63) | 4.26 (5.53) | 6.73 | |
| 1,000 | Rust, threads | 2.44 (3.00) | 2.95 (3.36) | 0.68 (0.77) | 1.65 (2.19) | 5.98 | |
| 2,000 | Node | 14.72 (22.79) | 12.41 (14.64) | 11.30 (17.76) | 23.04 (44.13) | 35.72 | 34.43 (54.97) |
| 2,000 | Rust, 1 thread | 7.13 (9.09) | 8.61 (9.40) | 4.46 (5.00) | 7.97 (9.47) | 18.85 | |
| 2,000 | Rust, threads | 7.30 (9.28) | 8.72 (9.85) | 1.71 (1.88) | 2.92 (3.62) | 17.18 | |

What it says, at 2,000 units:
- **This port is about twice as fast as Node, not ten times.** Ported work per tick: 35.7 ms in Node, 18.9 ms in Rust on one thread, 17.2 ms with threads. Rust's whole tick averages 17.1 ms with a p95 of 21.6, inside the 50 ms tick; Node's frozen `step()` averages 34.4 ms with a p95 of 55.0, over it.
- **Vision and fog gain most:** fog is 2.9 times faster on one thread and 7.9 times with a thread per team; vision 2.5 times, and 6.6 times threaded.
- **Movement gains 2.1 times and separation 1.4 times,** and neither uses threads, because each unit's step reads where the units before it ended up. Spatial-index lookups take about 36% of Rust's time (one `BTreeMap` lookup per candidate unit). Storing units by slot is the obvious next step, and the plan's group pathfinding and parallel tick (`DESIGN.md`, "Thousands of units") are the larger ones.
- **The parity result:** the vision and fog passes give the JavaScript's answers exactly on all four states; movement and separation match call by call over 200 ticks of each frozen state (646,400 calls, 1,360,000 unit states); the math matches x64 Node bit for bit. Whole ticks are not compared, because the port leaves out code that also moves units (`PARITY.md`, "How parity is judged").

## Match-owned observed routing (2026-10-12)

`match_routing.rs` initializes one owner-bound `RecipientRouting` after Conquest bases are placed. `Match::refresh_recipient_routing(owner)` returns before consumers borrow the recipient's immutable `query`, `knowledge` and `live` accessors separately from mutable game and search fields. Snapshot terrain reads share the same ordered memory and drain only its outbox. Cell and ruined-footprint discovery uses the source's current-position `teamSees`: exact `visionOf` distance, airborne bypass or a building's sight circle, otherwise existing terrain/smoke/height LOS. The Match grid is rebuilt before these queries. Live footprints use the current player visible-unit membership; mine ownership and discovery use canonical World maps. Fog masks filter live fires only, as the reference path view does.

The collector's terrain/vision key and the route view's tick/terrain/vision key remain separate. Visible unit membership is captured at the route key. Existing members' position, kind and HP refresh between same-key searches, matching the source's retained unit-object references. `retain_removed_route_unit` records a lifecycle owner's final removed Unit fields, including retained footprint owner/position, before the next route query. Source removal does not immediately discard an existing view's object. The command and tick owners consume this API independently; the module does not run their phases.

Recipient wreck memory preserves first-insertion order and rounded 0.1 m positions from the source rows. Current-visible wrecks replace remembered rows. Unseen removals stay remembered until their stored location is seen; hidden moving rows lose their motion and become static remembered cover. Snapshot wreck reads refresh full memory independently, while route wrecks remain cached at the route key. This projects only the fields the current core uses: canonical ID, position and motion presence. Missing or duplicate wreck IDs are rejected before collection. Type, owner, rotation and debris-row serialization remain outside this query representation. Same-key routing refresh reads live wetness because the source view retains the match's mutable weather object; roads remain a copied, key-gated value.

The original-source recorder constructs actual Conquest state and runs original visibility, fog, terrain and wreck collectors. A row-identity observation probe records accepted-row revision order without changing decisions. Four traces compare 162 actions, 44 route views, 16 terrain reads and seven wreck reads. Eight actual searches match waypoint/endpoint bits and expansion counts, and four cover queries check remembered wrecks. Cases cover ordinary and fog visibility, building circles, smoke, unfinished lookouts, weather, represented plane observer states, mines, unseen removal, footprints, teams, same-key live hazards and wetness. Focused tests pass in debug/release, the existing startup fixture passes all 62 cases, and strict core/simtest Clippy and formatting pass. Independent review found same-key wetness staleness; the fix and regression searches are included.

Recipient state is eagerly initialized after base placement, while the reference constructs routing projections on demand. Both use public authored terrain as the ordinary-map baseline and identical accepted memory rows afterward. No scaling or timing claim is made. Command, recruitment, convoy and tick phases consume this state in their own integration lanes. World memory, wire/ABI changes, new privacy policies and a full match claim are excluded. MOV-29 and MAP-100 remain in progress.

## Produced ground-unit placement

`produced_units.rs` ports the ground branches of `produceUnit` and `stepOut`. It borrows ordered current units, the production building and player spawn/rally facts, plus explicitly supplied authoritative terrain. The caller supplies the next unit ID, scatter index and tick, validates recruitment, and owns wallet changes and unit/grid registration. A failure returns no unit and consumes no allocator state.

Door placement tests 30 candidates in the source order: six spots per ring, with alternating heading offsets, and one hull further out per ring. Ground vehicles use the existing conservative turning-clearance view; other profiles use their ordinary movement mask. The overlap check ignores the new unit, aircraft, carried units and dead units, and only considers units close enough to the door. When an origin is supplied, candidates remain in its exact movement region. The final fallback deliberately keeps the source's weaker behavior: it can overlap units, and an origin-restricted fallback uses ordinary terrain rather than inflated vehicle terrain. A blocked fallback is retained when no free cell exists.

`SpawnPlacer::region_membership` reuses its existing component traversal for the origin check, including the source's common blocked label. No second region algorithm or vehicle-clearance implementation was added. The placed unit has the source heading and spawn ghost. Aim is returned separately because the current canonical Unit has no firing-aim field. The helper reuses `initial_unit` for fields currently represented by Unit; ability, combat, logistics and full air-base state remain the integrator's responsibility. Aircraft return `UnsupportedAir`.

A building rally takes precedence over a player rally when choosing the exit heading. The result carries an explicit building/player rally request and sets the represented world goal and rally drift. The caller must route from the placed unit and replace its path, even if the route is empty. The helper does not execute pathfinding or silently discard the rally order. Player rallies apply only in the source's skirmish base modes and not to structures; building rallies still apply in construction modes.

The recorder exports the unchanged pinned JavaScript functions and compares 934 door/production projections on 43 terrains, including all 44 non-air unit kinds, all 33 authored maps, live/dead/carried/air occupancy, crowded exits, occupied production buildings, river and cliff separation, blocked fallbacks and one-cell maps. The suite compares position, heading, aim, ghost, health and rally state; it does not claim the resulting route waypoints, wallet admission, full unit initialization or spatial insertion are integrated.

## Private authored maps for native matches (2026-10-12)

The server now embeds the 33 original authoring documents in a catalogue separate from its HTTP projections. `authoritative_maps::load` validates the original bytes through the core and caches immutable maps in `OnceLock`; `create_match` constructs fresh canonical Match/World state from that trusted source. Names select catalogue entries and never filesystem paths. This makes native authored-match construction independent of Node and the reference checkout at runtime.

The private generator verifies the exact clean reference pin and preserves every original JSON property, including mines, materials, triggers and scenario metadata. The public generator still calls `mapForClient`. HTTP routes can only use their own public index, including when an editor header is present. Embedding private authoring data is server packaging, not a new client contract or raw editor endpoint.

Tests construct a Conquest Match from every authored map, retain the 246 El Alamein mine cells and original triggers, and verify that mutation in one match changes neither a second match nor its cached source. The existing public-map checks and live HTTP harness remain the delivery/privacy evidence. This does not enable room simulation, logistics startup, generated World matches, saves or editor writes.

## Recipient unit delta ledger (2026-10-13)

The server owns one ledger per recipient stream. It consumes already filtered scalar unit rows and preserves the pinned `unitDelta` comparison and insertion-order removals. JSON integer and floating-point spellings compare as JavaScript numbers; held XOR is signed 32-bit. A shorter row with an otherwise identical prefix retains the source's quiet-delta behavior. A full request still replaces that row. The ledger owns its copies, so caller mutation cannot change previously sent state.

Whole-frame validation runs before any ledger mutation or resync consumption. Duplicate IDs, nonscalar columns and malformed IDs return errors. The ordinary start/reconnect reset clears held rows; the owner can then request full when the source role requires it. This primitive neither chooses visibility nor starts rooms. It does not implement shared wreck/node list versions, backpressure, terrain/fog production, role permission or the snapshot transport.

The original-source recorder passes 68 traces and 409 frames. Sixty-six traces consume actual seat snapshots from all 33 authored maps; two cover insertion history, signed XOR, full empty frames, reset and scalar tails. Independent source review and a separate recorder rerun are clear. Native verification remains pending.

## Snapshot list delivery history (2026-10-13)

Ordinary rooms capture raw wreck/node list versions once per immutable send-cache identity. Each recipient stream remembers those shared versions. World streams instead compare their own filtered list content. Reset clears unit and list history together; a full resync sends both. The delivery layer consumes an owned snapshot object so unchanged unit rows are not copied into an outgoing object. Role filtering and terrain/fog production remain the producer's responsibility.

The prototype compares JSON strings for lists. Native equality compares JSON content with JavaScript binary64 number equality, preserving negative-zero and integer/float equivalence while ignoring object key insertion order. Reordering otherwise identical object properties no longer causes a redundant resend. This is an internal cache choice; list payloads, array order and role filtering are unchanged. Shared versions remain authoritative outside World, including when one recipient's remembered list changes while the raw cache stays unchanged.

List shape/depth/size validation precedes recipient mutation, and both shared version increments are checked before either is committed. Existing core unit/cell limits bound list sizes and value traversal, with a depth guard of 128 levels. The original-source recorder matches seven traces and 75 steps, including shared cache identity, per-recipient World lists, late/full/reset streams, missing lists, wreck motion and overwriting stale delta flags. This slice does not start rooms, implement backpressure or alter the JSON/C contracts. Native verification remains pending.

## Snapshot write backpressure (2026-10-13)

Each socket owns its write accounting and owed-frame latch. Before producing a seat snapshot, the owner checks the source threshold of 256 × 1024 units. The JavaScript counts encoded string length rather than bytes, so the Rust helper counts UTF-16 units, including supplementary Unicode characters. A skipped send leaves all unit, terrain and fog delivery history untouched. A successful write reservation records its length until the transport reports completion, including any compression queue time.

Write tickets belong to the state that created them; callbacks must return to that same socket state after reconnect. Completing an old socket's write drains its accounting without scheduling a frame on the replacement socket. When the current socket drops below the limit, it consumes the owed latch before checking seat membership and room/game state. A valid catch-up asks the owner for empty shots/cells, a fresh snapshot cache and the existing delta history. Duplicate completions and exhausted counters fail without partial updates.

Watcher admission uses the original strict greater-than 4 MiB buffered threshold and close code 1013 with reason `too far behind`. Both ordinary shared streams and individual World watcher streams use that decision. The original server callback recorder matches 13 traces and 54 steps, plus 12 watcher cases run through both mode branches. This component has no sockets, timers or room simulation. Native checks and actual transport hookup remain pending.

## Host tick timing and snapshot cadence (2026-10-13)

The server timing components consume explicit host timestamps and measured durations. They do not read a clock inside the rules core. The catch-up clock starts due immediately, advances in 50 ms steps, runs at most four due ticks per poll, and drops any remaining backlog before scheduling the next step. Backwards host timestamps leave the deadline unchanged. Checked deadlines reject exhausted capacity before mutation.

Each room's meter keeps 200 samples for six phases and the last 50 snapshot tick durations. Snapshot phase samples are recorded on scheduled snapshot ticks, including those with no eligible sockets. Percentiles use the source's ordered ranks. After ten snapshot samples, a check each second raises the interval above a 40 ms p95, up to four ticks. A p95 below 24 ms for ten seconds lowers it one step, down to two ticks. A value equal to either threshold resets the recovery window. A decided winner requests a snapshot on every tick without changing the meter.

Timing controls transport cadence only; the AI retains its previously recorded fixed schedule and vision beat. Non-finite or negative durations and exhausted check deadlines fail before any history changes. The original default meter and unchanged host polling loop match eight traces with 747 samples and seven traces with 34 polls, including ring wraps and exact stall/threshold boundaries. At 315f46a, 23 server debug tests and all 173 release workspace tests pass, together with strict workspace Clippy, formatting and release FFI. The same sequence retains the delta, list and backpressure tests. Fresh core compilation followed package cleanup in both profiles. Actual room-loop callbacks and diagnostic logging remain pending.

## Canonical combat metadata (2026-10-13)

`Match.combat` owns XP, last-hit owner, death impulse, incoming shooter/time, anti-tank hit time and ordered cargo/carrier identities. Health, suppression, position, path and garrison stay in `World.game`. `Game.riding` is the query flag kept in sync with the canonical carrier identity. Starting units and bases initialize the source defaults. Removed shooter metadata may remain until the firing producer releases its last projectile reference, preserving source XP after shooter removal. Firing keeps target/cooldown/retarget and supplies an infallible `RetargetSink`; aim and command switches belong to the separate canonical UnitControl. `Match.environment` owns the reviewed terrain/fire/debris runtime. These fields reserve internal producer ownership; they do not establish combat phase completion or change the ABI.

## Conquest weapon firing producer (2026-10-13)

`Match.firing` owns chosen targets, cooldown and retarget timers, immutable round launch solutions, physical salvo timers and shared suppression identities. It reads command aim/intent and canonical combat XP/hit history, while World retains health and position. The tick caller captures prior poses after structure/grid synchronization and before advancing the clock or moving environment bodies, then builds claims before the live unit loop, decrements each unit's weapon timers before movement, then runs its firing block after movement and grid update. The timer does not duplicate suppression, sprint or path timers.

The targeting query uses current team visibility, ammo eligibility, naval hull reach, standing footprint cells and authoritative line of sight. Target scoring retains roles, cover, armor, incoming-fire preference, volley claims and switching hysteresis. Launching preserves setup and moving-fire guards, per-model counts, suppression accuracy/rate, cover, elevation and veterancy. Direct shots share one suppression identity per volley; artillery rounds retain delayed launches and their fixed duration. Physical salvo bookkeeping retains the original two otherwise unused random draws when its timer emits a segment. The recorded Combat stream isolates these draws from other match purposes; the prototype global stream is not claimed as a replay contract. Internal shot JSON normalizes signed zero to its JavaScript serialization value while retaining physical trajectory signs.

An unchanged-source loader checks 114 assembled arena traces and 588 checkpoints under x64 Node 24.21.0. The Rust replay compares float bits, target/claim order, aim and hull rotation, cooldowns, represented launch/contact fields, HP, suppression, XP, terrain, events and Combat stream state. Three focused tests pass in debug and release; all 155 workspace tests, strict all-targets Clippy, formatting and the release FFI build pass. The flight driver now samples traveled segments, moving bodies and naval hulls, applies shared near-miss suppression and typed unit/section contacts, removes terminal rounds before their blast effects, and retains removed shooter metadata while any round still refers to it. Terrain collision reads current World material names and their world-layer response values; unit contacts retain the original projectile steel response. Native replay covers delayed and direct impacts, moving bodies, glancing rebound, expiry, ammo shortages and retained shooter XP. Other modes, aircraft, rail, FPS operatives, beams, active abilities and terrain-fire orders remain outside this ordinary weapon phase. No ABI, wire or reference pin changed.

For exact equal-fraction unit collisions, Rust selects the lower numeric unit ID after terrain candidates. Grid bucket order and registration history cannot change that tie. Strict unequal-fraction comparisons and terrain precedence remain unchanged. This narrow choice follows the repository's defined-ID tie rule and is deliberately tested independently of exact prototype equality. The source recorder exercises ordinary collision outcomes, including glancing rebound, delayed salvo impacts, moving targets, walls and expiry. Geometry stress cases adjust flight velocity or target placement explicitly between phases; they establish driver behavior, not a full unmodified match outcome.

Recruitment publishes default firing rows alongside canonical command/combat state, and rejects an existing next-ID firing row before payment. The public execution hooks accept Default and Conquest only when canonical `Game.mode` is absent, and reject other modes or inconsistent internal mode state before looking up commander slots. World local defenders therefore require the future World actor accessor rather than a fake commander. Live flight payloads and physical salvo rows are checked before iteration mutation. Round contact processing follows source effect order; it is not a rollback transaction for an entire battle tick. No full World or unit-array clone occurs on the firing path.

## Ordinary firing mode aliases (2026-10-13)

The source `createGame` treats an omitted mode and explicit Conquest as ordinary games without a mode-specific state. Native startup already accepts both `Mode::Default` and `Mode::Conquest`, constructing `Game.mode = None`. Firing now admits those same aliases and checks that internal mode consistency before any execution. Other Match modes and either ordinary alias paired with a mode-specific `Game.mode` remain unsupported before writes or commander access.

The existing source replay tests run all 114 ordinary firing traces and all 30 typed ability-flight traces through both aliases, comparing each with the same recorded source state, events, float bits and random stream. The refusal regression covers every genuine other mode and all six inconsistent internal mode kinds, preserving world, commands, combat, firing, ammo and randomness. Native verification of this follow-up is pending its build lease; no C or wire change is involved.

## Typed ability-flight launch (2026-10-13)

`AbilityBlast::from_unit` reads the actual grenade/satchel ability values from the compiled unit catalog. Each `LiveFlight` retains its typed `ExplosionSource`, including the ability's infantry, vehicle, suppression and terrain values, instead of recovering a weapon profile from the shooter kind. This preserves medium-tank HE and Churchill/ranger/commando satchel behavior and rifle grenades. Ordinary direct weapon flights retain their absent blast source. The weapon salvo path is shared with barrage, including an omitted rocket barrage shell count, partial ammunition, scatter, delayed IDs and the physical salvo timer.

`launch_ability_flight` accepts an explicit target, typed blast, optional duration and optional ammunition debit. It checks geometry, unit facts, ammunition and ID capacity before mutation and returns the committed flight ID. A grenade launch consumes no Combat draws and emits Flight before Throw. `launch_weapon_salvo` stages its two Combat draws per round before committing ammunition, stream state, flights and the Salvo event. Ability/FPS callers own payment, cooldowns, command intent and operative counters, updating them after the successful launch. FPS supplies its source duration and the rifle ability profile, without a squad ammunition debit.

Firing owns the ordered physical grenade tickets (point, owner, remaining fuse and the same flight ID). `advance_firing_grenades` runs after flight contact resolution and before physical salvo timers, decrements the fuse and removes expired tickets or tickets whose flight was removed. It never produces a second blast. Unlinked legacy grenade payloads are not accepted because no supported caller requires them. The generic damage helper and captured blast-membership patch retain their separate owners.

The unchanged-source oracle runs the actual ability launch fragment, `launchSalvo`, `throwOperative`, flight/contact/blast driver and late ticket filter. Its 30 traces and 210 checkpoints compare launch geometry, retained profiles, damage, terrain, events, ammunition, IDs and stream state, including garrison height, partial barrage ammunition and three FPS durations. Both ability-flight tests and the three ordinary firing tests pass in debug and release. On compiled head `e1e74ad`, all 162 locked workspace release tests, strict workspace Clippy, formatting and the release FFI build pass after explicit release-package cleanup. The tests cover atomic rejection of malformed launch requests, metadata, ammunition and ID capacity. Full ability admission/payment, FPS admission/movement and tick attachment remain separate integration work. No C interface, wire contract or reference pin changes.

## Combat effect application and retirement (2026-10-13)

`match_combat::DamageWorld` borrows the canonical World, CombatState, maintained spatial grid, compiled rules, reserves/research and environment seed/ID owners. Direct-hit and suppression adapters compute the existing typed combat component results, preflight every event and rider placement, then apply them in source order. They update HP/suppression/position in Game and XP/hit/death/cargo identities in CombatState; a `RetargetSink` changes the firing producer's own timer. Suppression membership is an explicit shared volley set. Removed shooter metadata remains available until the firing producer releases its last projectile reference.

The local-contact adapter applies building slit/veterancy and shared section HP rules, preserving contact IDs and source XP. Terminal blasts apply the original ordinary-unit hurt formula and ordered rider unloading, then canonical terrain damage. Shots return as typed environment effects. The firing producer owns projectile launch/motion/contact selection, cover sampling and terminal removal order. No independent RNG stream is created by effect application; fire/debris operations borrow the existing shared environment seed. These adapters currently accept Conquest and represented ground entities.

`retire_dead` checks the captured tick order and canonical attribution, wallet, terrain/debris inputs before removing units. It preserves maintained grid bucket order through an explicit removal operation. Only actually removed units are cloned as returned retirement records; live-unit preflight uses borrowed facts. Buildings fail their existing sections and retain historical footprint ownership, while moving vehicles produce source-distinct wreck bodies. Game wreck coordinates/cells remain the query state and an optional durable unit ID connects them to body/type/owner/rotation metadata. The environment reconciles metadata when a terrain replacement removes a settled hull. Motion, active-body overflow, scorch, the 40-hull cap and final Q placement follow the original lifecycle. No living-unit health is copied into wreck state.

Story/fallen, FPS/companion, convoy and remembered-routing owners consume the returned records in source order. Story and companion admission must be preflighted before generic removal; routing retention must finish before the next route refresh. Kill bounty is paid separately from story and remains independent of winner gating, as in the source. Air, operative runtime, thrown rider escape, full convoy scheduling, command retirement and complete tick orchestration remain outside this adapter's completion claim.

The recorder executes the original direct damage, suppression, local contact, blast, section synchronization and late death block without editing the pinned reference checkout. Nine traces record 35 actions, including zero-damage rider unloading with missing/repeated cargo, retained shooter XP, shared suppression identity, building breach and synchronization, demolition, ordered deaths, killer exclusion and the wreck cap. All three focused tests pass in debug and release, including exact native fixture comparison, atomic invalid-input rejection and maintained removal order. The combined main-merge head passes all 131 workspace tests, strict workspace/all-targets Clippy and formatting. Finite expired sprint timers remain canonical negatives, with only the pure helper projection treating them as inactive. Terminal blasts carry an explicit optional direction into terrain debris. No full combat tick or match-outcome claim is made at this milestone.
## Match command state

`Match.commands.units` owns the fields absent from `Game::Unit`: aim, automatic ability/retreat switches, attack intent, following, facing and waiting orders. Existing routes, positions and hold-position state remain on Unit. Dynamically selected target and firing timers belong to firing state; hit memory and XP belong to combat state. A prepared `UnitControl` can be inserted without failure after recruitment commits its wallet transaction. Conquest ability switches use the source free-ability default; Classic ability costs remain outside the Conquest constructor. The typed command enum is an internal recorded input, not a new wire schema.

## Match Conquest recruitment (2026-10-13)

The native Match now stages immediate Conquest ground/naval recruitment through canonical production, wallets, IDs, unit insertion, grid updates and shared command controls. Owner-bound remembered routing is required for rallies; unreachable searches still admit an empty path as in the source. Air placement and pending supports without exact kind metadata fail explicitly before payment. [Recruitment design and evidence](docs/runtime/recruitment.md) records the transaction, control ownership, original-source fixture and remaining boundaries. No public interface or server match admission changed.

## Prepared unit publication (2026-10-13)

[Prepared publication](docs/runtime/unit-publication.md) gives recruitment, FPS and later unit actuators one disjoint-field transaction for units, spatial index, controls and IDs. Whole batches and optional existing HP changes validate before payment, then publish with no fallible operation. Metadata owners supply only infallible publication callbacks after their own checks. An explicit World actor factory and publication policy admit the fixed local defender without weakening ordinary commander checks or creating a wallet. The source-only prerequisite is pending verification.

## Match-owned observed routing (work in progress, 2026-10-12)

`match_routing.rs` initializes one owner-bound `RecipientRouting` after Conquest bases are placed. `Match::refresh_recipient_routing(owner)` returns before consumers borrow the recipient's immutable `query`, `knowledge` and `live` accessors separately from mutable game and search fields. Snapshot terrain reads share the same ordered memory and drain only its outbox. Cell and ruined-footprint discovery uses the source's current-position `teamSees`: exact `visionOf` distance, airborne bypass or a building's sight circle, otherwise existing terrain/smoke/height LOS. The Match grid is rebuilt before these queries. Live footprints use the current player visible-unit membership; mine ownership and discovery use canonical World maps. Fog masks filter live fires only, as the reference path view does.

The collector's terrain/vision key and the route view's tick/terrain/vision key remain separate. Visible unit membership is captured at the route key. Existing members' position, kind and HP refresh between same-key searches, matching the source's retained unit-object references. `retain_removed_route_unit` records a lifecycle owner's final removed Unit fields, including retained footprint owner/position, before the next route query. Source removal does not immediately discard an existing view's object. The command and tick owners consume this API independently; the module does not run their phases.

Recipient wreck memory preserves first-insertion order and rounded 0.1 m positions from the source rows. Current-visible wrecks replace remembered rows. Unseen removals stay remembered until their stored location is seen; hidden moving rows lose their motion and become static remembered cover. Snapshot wreck reads refresh full memory independently, while route wrecks remain cached at the route key. This projects only the fields the current core uses: canonical ID, position and motion presence. Missing or duplicate wreck IDs are rejected before collection. Type, owner, rotation and debris-row serialization remain outside this query representation. Same-key routing refresh reads live wetness because the source view retains the match's mutable weather object; roads remain a copied, key-gated value.

The original-source recorder constructs actual Conquest state and runs original visibility, fog, terrain and wreck collectors. A row-identity observation probe records accepted-row revision order without changing decisions. Four traces compare 162 actions, 44 route views, 16 terrain reads and seven wreck reads. Eight actual searches match waypoint/endpoint bits and expansion counts, and four cover queries check remembered wrecks. Cases cover ordinary and fog visibility, building circles, smoke, unfinished lookouts, weather, represented plane observer states, mines, unseen removal, footprints, teams, same-key live hazards and wetness. Focused tests pass in debug/release, the existing startup fixture passes all 60 cases, and strict core/simtest Clippy and formatting pass. Independent review found same-key wetness staleness; the fix and regression searches are included.

Recipient state is eagerly initialized after base placement, while the reference constructs routing projections on demand. Both use public authored terrain as the ordinary-map baseline and identical accepted memory rows afterward. No scaling or timing claim is made. Command, recruitment, convoy and tick phases consume this state in their own integration lanes. World memory, wire/ABI changes, new privacy policies and a full match claim are excluded. MOV-29 and MAP-100 remain in progress.
